Analogy. Softmax runs an election: each candidate's vote share grows with its score, and the shares add up to 100%. Temperature sets how decisive the voters are; at low temperature the leader takes most of the votes.

Set four logits and the temperature. Then push the shared offset past 709: the naive formula breaks, while the shifted version gives the same probabilities as before.

In ML: classifier outputs, attention weights () and token sampling () use softmax. Frameworks fuse softmax with cross-entropy and apply this trick for you; apply it yourself when you compute log-probabilities by hand.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def logsumexp(z):
    m = z.max()
    return m + np.log(np.exp(z - m).sum())     # shift by the max: nothing overflows

def softmax(z, T=1.0):
    z = np.asarray(z, dtype=float) / T
    e = np.exp(z - z.max())
    return e / e.sum()

z = np.array([2.0, 1.0, 0.1, -1.0])
print(softmax(z), softmax(z, T=0.2), softmax(z, T=5))
print(softmax(z + 1000), logsumexp(z + 1000))   # still fine at large scores
Ready to use Python · SciPy + PyTorch
import numpy as np
import torch
from scipy.special import logsumexp, softmax

z = np.array([2.0, 1.0, 0.1, -1.0]) + 1000
print(softmax(z), logsumexp(z))
t = torch.tensor(z)
print(torch.softmax(t, dim=0), torch.logsumexp(t, dim=0), torch.log_softmax(t, dim=0))

Monte Carlo estimation Intermediate

Many integrals and expectations have no closed form. Monte Carlo replaces them with averages over random samples:

$$\mathbb E_{x\sim p}[g(x)] \approx \frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N g(x_i), \qquad x_i \sim p$$

The estimate is unbiased, and by the CLT () its error shrinks like $1/\sqrt N$ whatever the dimension. A grid needs exponentially many points as dimensions grow, so sampling wins in high dimensions. Importance sampling draws from a different distribution $q$ and reweights: $\mathbb E_p[g] = \mathbb E_q\big[g(x)\,p(x)/q(x)\big]$.

Example. Throw $N$ points uniformly into the square $[-1, 1]^2$. The fraction inside the unit circle estimates $\pi/4$, so four times that fraction estimates $\pi$.

Analogy. To measure a pond on a map, scatter rice grains over the map at random and count the share that lands in the pond. More grains give a better estimate.

Add points. The right panel tracks the estimate of π as $N$ grows (log scale), inside the gray band $\pm 2\,\text{SE}$.

In ML: the reparameterization trick (), policy gradients (), diffusion training () and MC dropout estimate expectations by sampling.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
for n in (100, 10_000, 1_000_000):
    pts = rng.uniform(-1, 1, size=(n, 2))
    inside = (pts ** 2).sum(1) <= 1
    est = 4 * inside.mean()
    se = 4 * inside.std() / np.sqrt(n)            # error shrinks like 1/sqrt(n)
    print(n, est, f"±{2 * se:.4f}")

# E[g(X)] for X ~ N(0, 1), g(x) = x^2 (true value 1):
print((rng.standard_normal(100_000) ** 2).mean())
Ready to use Python · SciPy (quasi-Monte Carlo)
import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import qmc

sobol = qmc.Sobol(d=2, scramble=True, seed=0)       # low-discrepancy points fill space evenly
pts = sobol.random_base2(m=14) * 2 - 1              # 2^14 points in [-1, 1]^2
print(4 * ((pts ** 2).sum(1) <= 1).mean(), np.pi)

Markov chains Advanced

A Markov chain hops between states, and the next state depends only on the current one. A transition matrix $P$ with $P_{ij} = P(\text{next} = j \mid \text{now} = i)$ describes it, and each row sums to 1. If the row vector $\boldsymbol\pi_t$ holds the state probabilities at step $t$, then

$$\boldsymbol\pi_{t+1} = \boldsymbol\pi_t P$$

If you can reach each state from each other state and the chain doesn't cycle with a fixed period, $\boldsymbol\pi_t$ converges to a unique stationary distribution $\boldsymbol\pi^* = \boldsymbol\pi^* P$ from any start. $\boldsymbol\pi^*$ is a left eigenvector of $P$ with eigenvalue 1.

Analogy. A board game where your next square depends on your current square and the dice, and your earlier path doesn't matter. Play long enough and the share of turns you spend on each square settles, whatever square you started on.

Weather with three states. Set how likely each state is to repeat (the rest splits in half between the other two). Start from any state and step: the probabilities converge to the same $\boldsymbol\pi^*$.

In ML: an MDP () is a Markov chain with actions and rewards. MCMC samplers build a chain whose stationary distribution is the posterior you want. The forward process of a diffusion model () is a Markov chain from data to noise. PageRank is the stationary distribution of a random web surfer.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

P = np.array([[0.7, 0.15, 0.15],                    # rows: from Sunny, Cloudy, Rainy
              [0.3, 0.4, 0.3],
              [0.25, 0.25, 0.5]])
pi = np.array([1.0, 0.0, 0.0])                      # start: Sunny
for t in range(50):
    pi = pi @ P                                     # π_{t+1} = π_t P
print(pi, pi @ P)                                   # stationary: π = π P
Ready to use Python · NumPy (eigenvector)
import numpy as np

P = np.array([[0.7, 0.15, 0.15], [0.3, 0.4, 0.3], [0.25, 0.25, 0.5]])
vals, vecs = np.linalg.eig(P.T)                     # left eigenvectors of P
v = np.real(vecs[:, np.argmin(np.abs(vals - 1))])  # the one with eigenvalue 1
print(v / v.sum())
print(np.linalg.matrix_power(P, 50)[0])             # rows of P^t converge to π

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

In float32, what does $1 + 10^{-8}$ evaluate to?

1. The gap between 1 and the next float32 is $\epsilon \approx 1.2\times10^{-7}$, so the $10^{-8}$ rounds away.

Why does one outlier weight hurt 4-bit quantization, and what limits the damage?

The scale $s = \max_i|w_i|/7$ grows with the outlier, so the ordinary weights round onto two or three levels. One scale per group of 32 to 128 weights confines the damage to the outlier's own group.

Why subtract the largest score before computing softmax?

Softmax doesn't change when you shift all scores by a constant, and after the shift the largest exponential is $e^0 = 1$, so nothing overflows.

A Monte Carlo estimate has error 0.01 with 10,000 samples. How many samples give error 0.001?

The error falls like $1/\sqrt N$, so you need 100 times more samples: 1,000,000.

Part II. Machine learning

Machine learning fits a function to data. You choose a model family $f_\theta$, a loss that scores its predictions, and an optimizer that adjusts $\theta$ to lower the loss on training data. In supervised learning each example $\mathbf x_i$ comes with a label $y_i$. In unsupervised learning you only have the $\mathbf x_i$ and look for structure in them.

Foundations of machine learning

Classification metrics and the ROC curve Beginner

A classifier outputs a score, and a threshold turns the score into a decision. Compare the decisions with the truth and you get four counts: true positives (TP), false positives (FP), true negatives (TN) and false negatives (FN). Together they form the confusion matrix.

$$\text{accuracy} = \frac{TP + TN}{\text{all}} \qquad \text{precision} = \frac{TP}{TP + FP} \qquad \text{recall} = \frac{TP}{TP + FN} \qquad F_1 = \frac{2PR}{P + R}$$

Precision is the share of flagged items that are real positives. Recall is the share of real positives you caught. Raise the threshold and you flag fewer items: precision tends to rise while recall falls. $F_1$, the harmonic mean, stays low unless both are high.

The ROC curve plots recall (the true positive rate) against the false positive rate $FP/(FP + TN)$ as the threshold sweeps from high to low. The area under it, ROC-AUC, equals the probability that a random positive outscores a random negative: 0.5 for coin flipping, 1 for a perfect ranking. With rare positives, prefer the precision–recall curve, since ROC can look fine while precision is poor.

For regression you use the mean squared error $\frac1n\sum(y_i - \hat y_i)^2$, the mean absolute error $\frac1n\sum|y_i - \hat y_i|$, and $R^2 = 1 - \frac{\sum(y_i - \hat y_i)^2}{\sum(y_i - \bar y)^2}$, the fraction of variance the model explains.

Analogy. A fishing net catches some of the fish you want plus some junk. Precision is the share of your catch that is the fish you wanted; recall is the share of those fish in the lake that you caught. A wider net raises recall and lowers precision.

Scores of negatives (blue) and positives (red), 500 of each. Move the threshold: the counts, the metrics and the point on the ROC curve follow. Increase the separation and the AUC climbs toward 1.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

y = np.array([1, 0, 1, 1, 0, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0])
score = np.array([0.9, 0.4, 0.8, 0.35, 0.3, 0.6, 0.7, 0.1, 0.55, 0.2])
pred = (score >= 0.5).astype(int)
tp = ((pred == 1) & (y == 1)).sum(); fp = ((pred == 1) & (y == 0)).sum()
fn = ((pred == 0) & (y == 1)).sum(); tn = ((pred == 0) & (y == 0)).sum()
precision, recall = tp / (tp + fp), tp / (tp + fn)
f1 = 2 * precision * recall / (precision + recall)
# AUC = P(random positive outscores random negative), ties count half
pos, neg = score[y == 1], score[y == 0]
auc = ((pos[:, None] > neg[None, :]).mean() + 0.5 * (pos[:, None] == neg[None, :]).mean())
print(tp, fp, fn, tn, precision, recall, round(f1, 3), auc)
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.metrics import classification_report, confusion_matrix, roc_auc_score, roc_curve

y = np.array([1, 0, 1, 1, 0, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0])
score = np.array([0.9, 0.4, 0.8, 0.35, 0.3, 0.6, 0.7, 0.1, 0.55, 0.2])
pred = (score >= 0.5).astype(int)
print(confusion_matrix(y, pred))                 # rows: actual 0, 1; columns: predicted 0, 1
print(classification_report(y, pred, digits=3))
print(roc_auc_score(y, score))
fpr, tpr, thresholds = roc_curve(y, score)       # points of the ROC curve

Feature scaling Beginner

Features often live on very different scales: house area runs from 50 to 500 square meters, while rooms run from 1 to 8. Two common fixes:

$$\text{standardization: } x' = \frac{x - \mu}{\sigma} \qquad\qquad \text{min-max: } x' = \frac{x - x_{\min}}{x_{\max} - x_{\min}}$$

Standardization gives each feature mean 0 and standard deviation 1. Compute $\mu$ and $\sigma$ on the training set only, then apply the same numbers to validation and test data, or you leak test information into training.

Scale matters in two places. Distance-based models (k-NN, k-means, SVMs with an RBF kernel) let the largest-scale feature dominate the distance. Gradient descent on unscaled features faces a long, narrow valley. The loss is steep along one weight and flat along the other, so a learning rate small enough for the steep direction crawls along the flat one. Tree models split on one feature at a time and ignore scale.

Analogy. Comparing a 50-square-meter difference with a 2-room difference is like adding grams to tons: the bigger unit drowns the smaller one. Standardizing converts each feature to the same currency.

Gradient descent on a linear-regression loss with two features. Without scaling, the second feature is 5 times larger and the valley is narrow. Tick "standardize" and the same learning rate reaches the minimum in a few steps.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X_train = np.column_stack([rng.uniform(50, 500, 100), rng.integers(1, 9, 100)])   # area, rooms
X_test = np.column_stack([rng.uniform(50, 500, 20), rng.integers(1, 9, 20)])
mu, sigma = X_train.mean(0), X_train.std(0)        # fit on training data only
Z_train, Z_test = (X_train - mu) / sigma, (X_test - mu) / sigma
lo, hi = X_train.min(0), X_train.max(0)
M_train = (X_train - lo) / (hi - lo)               # min-max to [0, 1]
print(Z_train.mean(0).round(6), Z_train.std(0), Z_test.mean(0), M_train.min(0), M_train.max(0))
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler, StandardScaler

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.column_stack([rng.uniform(50, 500, 200), rng.integers(1, 9, 200)])
y = (X[:, 0] / 100 + X[:, 1] > 7).astype(int)
scaler = StandardScaler().fit(X[:150])                 # learn mean and std from training rows
print(scaler.mean_, scaler.scale_, scaler.transform(X[150:153]))
model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression())   # the pipeline refits the scaler inside each CV fold
print(model.fit(X[:150], y[:150]).score(X[150:], y[150:]))
print(MinMaxScaler().fit_transform(X[:3]))

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

1% of patients have a disease, and a model predicts "healthy" for all of them. What are its accuracy and recall?

Accuracy 99% and recall 0: it finds none of the sick patients. On imbalanced data, report precision and recall, or the ROC and precision-recall curves.

Why do you compute the scaling mean and standard deviation on the training set alone?

Computing them on validation or test data leaks information from those sets into training and makes your scores optimistic. Fit the scaler on the training data and apply the same numbers to the rest.

Supervised learning

Linear regression Beginner

Linear regression predicts a number as a weighted sum of the features, $\hat y = \mathbf w^\top\mathbf x + b$. You fit it by minimizing the mean squared error. Stack the examples as rows of a matrix $X$, with a column of ones to absorb $b$, and the targets in a vector $\mathbf y$:

$$L(\mathbf w) = \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i)^2 = \frac1n\|\mathbf y - X\mathbf w\|^2$$

Set the gradient $\nabla L = -\frac2n X^\top(\mathbf y - X\mathbf w)$ to zero and you get the normal equations and their solution:

$$X^\top X\,\mathbf w = X^\top\mathbf y \qquad\Rightarrow\qquad \mathbf w^* = (X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top\mathbf y$$

Geometrically, $X\mathbf w^*$ is the projection of $\mathbf y$ onto the span of the feature columns, and the residual $\mathbf y - X\mathbf w^*$ is orthogonal to each column. With one feature the solution reads $w = \operatorname{Cov}(x, y)/\operatorname{Var}(x)$ and $b = \bar y - w\bar x$.

If you assume $y = \mathbf w^\top\mathbf x + \varepsilon$ with Gaussian noise $\varepsilon \sim \mathcal N(0, \sigma^2)$, minimizing MSE and maximizing the likelihood () give the same weights.

  1. Add a column of ones to $X$.
  2. Solve $X^\top X\,\mathbf w = X^\top\mathbf y$ with a least-squares solver (QR or SVD). Avoid forming the inverse. The cost is $O(nd^2 + d^3)$; for huge $n$ or $d$, use gradient descent.
  3. Check residuals and $R^2$ on held-out data.

Analogy. Lay a straight rod through a cloud of points and tie each point to the rod with a vertical spring. The rod settles where the total spring energy, the sum of squared residuals, is lowest. That resting place is the least-squares line.

Drag the points, or click empty space to add one. Blue is the least-squares line; the squares show each squared residual. Fit your own orange line with the sliders and try to match the blue MSE.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.uniform(0, 10, (50, 2))
y = 3 + 2 * X[:, 0] - X[:, 1] + rng.normal(0, 0.5, 50)
A = np.column_stack([np.ones(len(X)), X])           # column of ones for the bias b
w = np.linalg.solve(A.T @ A, A.T @ y)               # normal equations XᵀX w = Xᵀy
w_lstsq = np.linalg.lstsq(A, y, rcond=None)[0]      # numerically safer (QR/SVD)
resid = y - A @ w
r2 = 1 - (resid ** 2).sum() / ((y - y.mean()) ** 2).sum()
print(w, w_lstsq, round(r2, 4))

w_gd = np.zeros(3)                                  # same fit by gradient descent
for _ in range(20_000):
    w_gd -= 0.01 * (-2 / len(y)) * A.T @ (y - A @ w_gd)
print(w_gd)
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error, r2_score

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.uniform(0, 10, (50, 2))
y = 3 + 2 * X[:, 0] - X[:, 1] + rng.normal(0, 0.5, 50)
model = LinearRegression().fit(X, y)
print(model.intercept_, model.coef_)
pred = model.predict(X)
print(mean_squared_error(y, pred), r2_score(y, pred))

k-nearest neighbors Beginner

k-NN stores the training set and skips training. To classify a new point, find the $k$ training points closest to it and take a majority vote of their labels. For regression, average their targets:

$$\hat y(\mathbf x) = \operatorname{mode}\{\,y_i : i \in N_k(\mathbf x)\,\}, \qquad d(\mathbf x, \mathbf x') = \|\mathbf x - \mathbf x'\|_2$$

$k$ sets the bias–variance tradeoff (). With $k = 1$ the boundary hugs each training point and overfits. A large $k$ smooths the boundary, and at $k = n$ it predicts the majority class everywhere. Weighting each vote by $1/d$ gives close neighbors more say.

A query costs $O(nd)$ unless you index the data with k-d trees, ball trees, or approximate methods such as HNSW for embeddings. In high dimensions distances concentrate: the nearest and the farthest neighbor sit at similar distances, so k-NN needs good features or a learned embedding.

  1. Scale the features ().
  2. For a query $\mathbf x$, compute the distance to each training point.
  3. Take the $k$ nearest and vote, or average.

Analogy. To guess whether a house will sell above its asking price, look at the $k$ most similar houses that sold this year and go with the majority.

Slide $k$. At $k = 1$ the regions form islands around single points; larger $k$ smooths them. Click to add a blue point, shift-click for red.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def knn_predict(X_train, y_train, X_query, k=5):
    d2 = ((X_query[:, None, :] - X_train[None, :, :]) ** 2).sum(-1)   # squared distances
    nearest = np.argsort(d2, axis=1)[:, :k]
    votes = y_train[nearest]
    return np.array([np.bincount(v).argmax() for v in votes])         # majority vote

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal(-1, 0.6, (50, 2)), rng.normal(1, 0.6, (50, 2))])
y = np.repeat([0, 1], 50)
print(knn_predict(X, y, np.array([[-1.0, -1.0], [0.9, 1.2], [0.0, 0.1]]), k=5))
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal(-1, 0.6, (50, 2)), rng.normal(1, 0.6, (50, 2))])
y = np.repeat([0, 1], 50)
for k in (1, 5, 25):
    model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=k, weights="distance"))
    print(k, cross_val_score(model, X, y, cv=5).mean())

Logistic and softmax regression Intermediate

Logistic regression turns a linear score into a probability with the sigmoid:

$$P(y = 1 \mid \mathbf x) = \sigma(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x + b), \qquad \sigma(z) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-z}}$$

The decision boundary $\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x + b = 0$ is a line, or a hyperplane in more dimensions. The score equals the log-odds, $z = \log\frac{p}{1 - p}$. Treat each label as a Bernoulli draw and maximize the likelihood (); the negative log-likelihood is the binary cross-entropy:

$$L(\mathbf w) = -\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\Big[y_i\log p_i + (1 - y_i)\log(1 - p_i)\Big], \qquad p_i = \sigma(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b)$$

Since $\sigma' = \sigma(1 - \sigma)$, the gradient collapses to prediction minus label, times the input:

$$\nabla_{\mathbf w} L = \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n (p_i - y_i)\,\mathbf x_i$$

The loss is convex, so gradient descent or Newton's method () finds the global minimum. On separable data the weights grow without bound; an L2 penalty stops that. For $K$ classes, softmax regression gives each class a weight vector and normalizes with softmax ():

$$P(y = k \mid \mathbf x) = \frac{e^{\mathbf w_k^\top\mathbf x}}{\sum_j e^{\mathbf w_j^\top\mathbf x}}, \qquad \nabla_{\mathbf w_k}L = \frac1n\sum_i \big(p_{ik} - [y_i = k]\big)\,\mathbf x_i$$

Analogy. Logistic regression works like a dimmer switch. The linear score turns the knob, and the sigmoid maps the knob position to a brightness between 0 and 1: the probability.

Press Train. The background shows the predicted class, fading where the model is unsure. Click to add a blue point, shift-click for red. Try "xor": a linear boundary can't separate it.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal(-1, 0.8, (100, 2)), rng.normal(1, 0.8, (100, 2))])
y = np.repeat([0.0, 1.0], 100)
A = np.column_stack([X, np.ones(len(X))])
w, lr, lam = np.zeros(3), 0.5, 1e-3
sigmoid = lambda z: 1 / (1 + np.exp(-z))
for _ in range(2000):
    p = sigmoid(A @ w)
    grad = A.T @ (p - y) / len(y) + lam * np.r_[w[:2], 0]   # (p − y) x, plus L2 on the weights
    w -= lr * grad
p = sigmoid(A @ w)
loss = -np.mean(y * np.log(p) + (1 - y) * np.log(1 - p))
print(w, loss, ((p > 0.5) == y).mean())
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal(-1, 0.8, (100, 2)), rng.normal(1, 0.8, (100, 2)), rng.normal([2, -2], 0.8, (100, 2))])
y = np.repeat([0, 1, 2], 100)                       # three classes: softmax regression
model = LogisticRegression(C=1.0).fit(X, y)         # C = 1/λ, smaller C = stronger L2
print(model.coef_, model.intercept_)
print(model.predict_proba([[0.0, 0.0]]).round(3), model.score(X, y))

Ridge and Lasso regularization Intermediate

With many or correlated features, least squares overfits, and $X^\top X$ can come close to singular. Regularization adds a penalty on the size of the weights:

$$\text{Ridge: } \min_{\mathbf w}\ \|\mathbf y - X\mathbf w\|^2 + \lambda\|\mathbf w\|_2^2 \qquad\qquad \text{Lasso: } \min_{\mathbf w}\ \|\mathbf y - X\mathbf w\|^2 + \lambda\|\mathbf w\|_1$$

Ridge keeps a closed form, $\mathbf w^* = (X^\top X + \lambda I)^{-1}X^\top\mathbf y$. Adding $\lambda I$ lifts each eigenvalue by $\lambda$, which stabilizes the inverse. Lasso has no closed form; solvers use coordinate descent with soft-thresholding, $w_j \leftarrow \operatorname{sign}(\rho_j)\max(|\rho_j| - \lambda/2,\ 0)/\|X_{:,j}\|^2$ for $\lambda$ written as above.

Each penalty matches a constraint: minimize the loss subject to $\|\mathbf w\| \le t$. The solution is the first point where the loss contours (ellipses around the unregularized solution) touch the constraint region. The L1 region is a diamond with corners on the axes, and the ellipses tend to touch it at a corner, where one weight equals zero. Lasso therefore selects features. Ridge shrinks all weights toward zero but keeps them nonzero. Elastic net mixes both penalties.

Analogy. Regularization gives the model a budget, and each unit of weight costs money. L1 charges a flat price per unit, so the model drops features that don't pay their way. L2 charges more for big weights, so the model spreads its budget across features.

The contours show the squared error over two weights; the black cross marks the unregularized solution. Shrink the budget $t$ and watch where the contours first touch the region. With L1, the solution snaps to an axis and $w_2$ becomes 0.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.normal(size=(60, 5))
y = X @ np.array([3.0, 0.0, -2.0, 0.0, 0.5]) + rng.normal(0, 0.5, 60)
lam = 10.0
w_ridge = np.linalg.solve(X.T @ X + lam * np.eye(5), X.T @ y)   # (XᵀX + λI)⁻¹ Xᵀy

def lasso_cd(X, y, lam, iters=200):
    """Coordinate descent for ||y − Xw||² + λ||w||₁ with soft-thresholding."""
    w = np.zeros(X.shape[1])
    for _ in range(iters):
        for j in range(X.shape[1]):
            r = y - X @ w + X[:, j] * w[j]                         # residual without feature j
            rho = X[:, j] @ r
            w[j] = np.sign(rho) * max(abs(rho) - lam / 2, 0) / (X[:, j] @ X[:, j])
    return w

print(w_ridge.round(3))
print(lasso_cd(X, y, lam=60.0).round(3))            # exact zeros for the useless features
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.linear_model import ElasticNet, Lasso, LassoCV, Ridge

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.normal(size=(60, 5))
y = X @ np.array([3.0, 0.0, -2.0, 0.0, 0.5]) + rng.normal(0, 0.5, 60)
print(Ridge(alpha=10.0).fit(X, y).coef_.round(3))
print(Lasso(alpha=0.5).fit(X, y).coef_.round(3))     # sklearn scales the loss by 1/(2n)
print(ElasticNet(alpha=0.5, l1_ratio=0.5).fit(X, y).coef_.round(3))
print(LassoCV(cv=5).fit(X, y).alpha_)                # picks alpha by cross-validation

Naive Bayes Intermediate

Naive Bayes applies Bayes' theorem () with one bold simplification: given the class, it treats the features as independent.

$$P(y \mid \mathbf x) \;\propto\; P(y)\prod_{j=1}^d P(x_j \mid y)$$

Real features depend on each other, so the assumption is wrong, yet the classifier ranks classes well and trains in one pass over the data. Gaussian naive Bayes models each feature within each class as $\mathcal N(\mu_{jy}, \sigma_{jy}^2)$, using the class mean and variance. Multinomial naive Bayes for text uses word counts,

$$P(x_j \mid y) = \frac{\text{count}(j, y) + \alpha}{\sum_{j'}\text{count}(j', y) + \alpha d},$$

where $\alpha = 1$ (Laplace smoothing) stops an unseen word from zeroing the whole product. Compute in log space to avoid underflow:

$$\hat y = \arg\max_y \Big[\log P(y) + \sum_j \log P(x_j \mid y)\Big]$$

Analogy. A spam filter judges each word on its own: "free" raises the spam score, "meeting" lowers it. It multiplies the clues together without asking how the words relate, and it still sorts mail well.

Gaussian naive Bayes. The ellipses show each class's fitted Gaussian; they stay axis-aligned, because the model assumes independent features. The boundary is a curve (a quadratic), not a line.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal([-1, -1], 0.7, (60, 2)), rng.normal([1, 1], [1.0, 0.5], (60, 2))])
y = np.repeat([0, 1], 60)
classes = np.unique(y)
prior = np.array([(y == c).mean() for c in classes])
mu = np.array([X[y == c].mean(0) for c in classes])
var = np.array([X[y == c].var(0) for c in classes]) + 1e-9

def predict_log_proba(Xq):
    # log P(y) + Σ_j log N(x_j | μ_jy, σ²_jy), then normalize with log-sum-exp
    ll = -0.5 * (np.log(2 * np.pi * var)[None] + (Xq[:, None, :] - mu[None]) ** 2 / var[None]).sum(-1)
    joint = np.log(prior)[None] + ll
    return joint - np.logaddexp.reduce(joint, axis=1, keepdims=True)

print(np.exp(predict_log_proba(np.array([[0.0, 0.0], [1.0, 1.2]]))).round(3))
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import CountVectorizer
from sklearn.naive_bayes import GaussianNB, MultinomialNB

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal([-1, -1], 0.7, (60, 2)), rng.normal([1, 1], [1.0, 0.5], (60, 2))])
y = np.repeat([0, 1], 60)
print(GaussianNB().fit(X, y).predict_proba([[0.0, 0.0], [1.0, 1.2]]).round(3))

texts = ["win free money now", "free prize claim now", "meeting at noon", "project meeting notes"]
labels = [1, 1, 0, 0]                               # 1 = spam
vec = CountVectorizer()
nb = MultinomialNB(alpha=1.0).fit(vec.fit_transform(texts), labels)   # alpha = Laplace smoothing
print(nb.predict(vec.transform(["free money meeting"])), nb.predict_proba(vec.transform(["notes at noon"])).round(3))

Decision trees Intermediate

A decision tree asks a sequence of yes/no questions, such as "is $x_3 \le 2.5$?". Each leaf holds a prediction: the majority class, or the mean target for regression. You can read the path behind any prediction.

Trees grow one greedy split at a time. At each node you try each feature and each threshold, and you keep the split that leaves the children purest. For a node with class proportions $p_k$, two common impurity measures are

$$\text{Gini} = 1 - \sum_k p_k^2 \qquad\qquad \text{Entropy} = -\sum_k p_k\log_2 p_k$$

The gain of a split is the parent's impurity minus the size-weighted impurity of the children. With entropy, this is the information gain (). Regression trees use the variance of the targets as the impurity.

$$\Delta = I(\text{parent}) - \frac{n_L}{n}\,I(L) - \frac{n_R}{n}\,I(R)$$
Example. A node holds 5 red and 5 blue points: Gini $= 1 - 0.5^2 - 0.5^2 = 0.5$. A split into (4 red, 1 blue) and (1 red, 4 blue) gives each child Gini $= 1 - 0.8^2 - 0.2^2 = 0.32$, so $\Delta = 0.5 - 0.32 = 0.18$.

You stop at a maximum depth, at a minimum leaf size, or when no split helps. A deep tree memorizes the training set; you control that by pruning, or by averaging many trees (chapter ). Trees handle mixed feature types, need no scaling, and capture interactions. Their boundaries are axis-aligned staircases.

  1. Start with all data at the root.
  2. For each feature, sort the values and scan the thresholds between them, scoring each split by its gain.
  3. Split on the best (feature, threshold) and recurse into each child.
  4. Stop at the depth or leaf-size limit. The leaf predicts its majority class.

Analogy. A decision tree plays twenty questions. Each question splits the remaining cases, and the best question leaves the two groups as pure as possible.

Raise the depth one level at a time and watch the tree add axis-aligned cuts. Past depth 6 or so, it starts carving out single points.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def gini(y):
    p = np.bincount(y) / len(y)
    return 1 - (p ** 2).sum()

def best_split(X, y):
    best = (0.0, None, None)                      # (gain, feature, threshold)
    for j in range(X.shape[1]):
        for t in np.unique(X[:, j])[:-1]:
            left = X[:, j] <= t
            gain = gini(y) - left.mean() * gini(y[left]) - (~left).mean() * gini(y[~left])
            if gain > best[0]:
                best = (gain, j, t)
    return best

def grow(X, y, depth=0, max_depth=3, min_leaf=2):
    gain, j, t = best_split(X, y)
    if depth == max_depth or j is None or len(y) < 2 * min_leaf:
        return np.bincount(y).argmax()            # leaf: majority class
    left = X[:, j] <= t
    return (j, t, grow(X[left], y[left], depth + 1, max_depth), grow(X[~left], y[~left], depth + 1, max_depth))

def predict(node, x):
    while isinstance(node, tuple):
        j, t, l, r = node
        node = l if x[j] <= t else r
    return node

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.uniform(-1, 1, (200, 2))
y = ((X[:, 0] > 0) ^ (X[:, 1] > 0.3)).astype(int)
tree = grow(X, y)
print(np.mean([predict(tree, x) == t for x, t in zip(X, y)]))
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier, export_text

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.uniform(-1, 1, (200, 2))
y = ((X[:, 0] > 0) ^ (X[:, 1] > 0.3)).astype(int)
tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=3, criterion="gini", min_samples_leaf=2).fit(X, y)
print(export_text(tree, feature_names=["x0", "x1"]))
print(tree.score(X, y), tree.feature_importances_)

Support vector machines and kernels Advanced

Many lines can separate two classes. The support vector machine picks the one with the widest margin, the largest gap to the nearest points of either class. Use labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$ and scale $\mathbf w$ so the closest points satisfy $y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b) = 1$. The margin then equals $2/\|\mathbf w\|$, and maximizing it becomes:

$$\min_{\mathbf w, b}\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 \quad\text{subject to}\quad y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 \ \text{ for each } i$$

Real data overlaps, so the soft-margin SVM lets points violate the margin at a price $C$:

$$\min_{\mathbf w, b}\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i \max\big(0,\ 1 - y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b)\big)$$

The second term is the hinge loss. A large $C$ punishes violations hard and narrows the margin; a small $C$ tolerates them and widens it. The Lagrangian dual () touches the data only through dot products:

$$\max_{\boldsymbol\alpha}\ \sum_i\alpha_i - \tfrac12\sum_{i,j}\alpha_i\alpha_j y_i y_j\,\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf x_j \quad\text{s.t.}\quad 0 \le \alpha_i \le C,\ \ \sum_i\alpha_i y_i = 0$$

Only points on or inside the margin get $\alpha_i > 0$. These are the support vectors, and the rest of the data could vanish without changing the boundary. The kernel trick replaces each dot product with a kernel $k(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x_j) = \phi(\mathbf x_i)^\top\phi(\mathbf x_j)$, a dot product in a feature space you never compute. The RBF kernel $k(\mathbf x, \mathbf x') = \exp(-\gamma\|\mathbf x - \mathbf x'\|^2)$ corresponds to an infinite-dimensional space, and the classifier becomes

$$f(\mathbf x) = \sum_i \alpha_i y_i\,k(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x) + b$$

Analogy. Draw the widest possible street between two neighborhoods. The houses on the curbs are the support vectors: move any other house and the street stays where it is. The kernel trick works like separating red and blue marbles on a table by tossing them into the air and sliding a sheet between them.

Train the SVM (by the Pegasos subgradient method). The solid line is $f = 0$, the dotted lines are $f = \pm 1$, and the ringed points are support vectors. Lower $C$ and the margin widens. Switch to the RBF kernel for curved boundaries, and raise $\gamma$ to make them tighter.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def pegasos(X, y, C=1.0, iters=20_000, seed=0):
    """Linear SVM by stochastic subgradient descent on the hinge loss. y in {-1, +1}."""
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    n = len(y)
    lam = 1 / (C * n)
    w = np.zeros(X.shape[1] + 1)
    Xb = np.column_stack([X, np.ones(n)])          # bias as an extra input
    for t in range(1, iters + 1):
        i = rng.integers(n)
        eta = 1 / (lam * t)
        if y[i] * (Xb[i] @ w) < 1:                 # inside the margin: hinge is active
            w = (1 - eta * lam) * w + eta * y[i] * Xb[i]
        else:
            w = (1 - eta * lam) * w
    return w

rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal(-1.5, 0.7, (50, 2)), rng.normal(1.5, 0.7, (50, 2))])
y = np.repeat([-1, 1], 50)
w = pegasos(X, y)
margins = y * (np.column_stack([X, np.ones(100)]) @ w)
print(w, (margins > 0).mean(), "margin width", 2 / np.linalg.norm(w[:2]))
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.svm import SVC, LinearSVC
from sklearn.datasets import make_moons

X, y = make_moons(200, noise=0.15, random_state=0)
rbf = SVC(kernel="rbf", C=1.0, gamma=2.0).fit(X, y)
print(rbf.score(X, y), "support vectors:", rbf.n_support_)
lin = LinearSVC(C=1.0).fit(X, y)
print(lin.score(X, y), lin.coef_, lin.intercept_)
print(rbf.decision_function(X[:3]))              # f(x); the sign gives the class

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

Why does k-NN with $k = 1$ overfit, and what does a larger $k$ do?

With $k = 1$ each training point, noise included, claims its own neighborhood: zero training error and a jagged boundary. A larger $k$ averages more neighbors and smooths the boundary, trading variance for bias.

Where is logistic regression's decision boundary at threshold 0.5, and what shape does it have?

Where $\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x + b = 0$, since $\sigma(0) = 0.5$. It is a hyperplane: a straight line in two dimensions.

How does a decision tree choose a split?

It tries each feature and threshold and keeps the split that most reduces the impurity (Gini or entropy) of the two children, weighted by their sizes.

How can an SVM with an RBF kernel work in an infinite-dimensional feature space?

Training and prediction touch the data through dot products alone, and the kernel $k(\mathbf x, \mathbf x')$ computes the feature-space dot product from the original vectors, so the features themselves never enter the computation. This is the kernel trick.

Ensembles

Bagging and random forests Intermediate

Averaging reduces variance. Average $B$ models whose errors each have variance $\sigma^2$ and pairwise correlation $\rho$, and the variance of the average is

$$\operatorname{Var}\Big(\frac1B\sum_{b=1}^B f_b\Big) = \rho\,\sigma^2 + \frac{1 - \rho}{B}\,\sigma^2$$

More models shrink the second term. Less correlated models shrink the first. Bagging (bootstrap aggregating) trains each tree on a bootstrap sample, $n$ rows drawn with replacement. Each sample leaves out about $(1 - 1/n)^n \approx e^{-1} \approx 37\%$ of the rows, and those out-of-bag rows give you a free validation score.

A random forest cuts $\rho$ further: at each split, the tree may only choose among a random subset of $m$ features, often $m = \sqrt d$ for classification and $m = d/3$ for regression. The trees grow deep (low bias, high variance), and averaging cancels much of the variance. For feature importance, add up the impurity decrease of each feature's splits across all trees, or measure how much the out-of-bag score drops when you shuffle that feature (permutation importance).

  1. For $b = 1, \dots, B$: draw a bootstrap sample and grow a deep tree, choosing each split among $m$ random features.
  2. Predict with the average of the trees' class probabilities (or a majority vote).

Analogy. Ask 100 people to guess the number of beans in a jar: the average guess beats most individuals, as long as their errors don't all lean the same way. Random features keep the trees from copying each other's errors.

One tree gives a jagged boundary. Add trees and the average smooths it. The out-of-bag accuracy estimates test accuracy without a validation set.

From scratch Python · NumPy + scikit-learn tree
import numpy as np
from sklearn.datasets import make_moons
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier

X, y = make_moons(300, noise=0.3, random_state=0)
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
B, n = 100, len(y)
votes = np.zeros((n, 2))
trees = []
for b in range(B):
    idx = rng.integers(0, n, n)                                  # bootstrap sample
    tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_features="sqrt", random_state=b).fit(X[idx], y[idx])  # random features per split
    trees.append(tree)
    oob = np.setdiff1d(np.arange(n), idx)                        # rows this tree never saw
    votes[oob] += tree.predict_proba(X[oob])
predict = lambda Xq: np.mean([t.predict_proba(Xq) for t in trees], axis=0).argmax(1)
has_vote = votes.sum(1) > 0
print("train acc", (predict(X) == y).mean(), "OOB acc", (votes[has_vote].argmax(1) == y[has_vote]).mean())
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
from sklearn.datasets import make_moons
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.inspection import permutation_importance

X, y = make_moons(300, noise=0.3, random_state=0)
rf = RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=200, max_features="sqrt", oob_score=True, random_state=0).fit(X, y)
print(rf.oob_score_, rf.feature_importances_)
print(permutation_importance(rf, X, y, n_repeats=5, random_state=0).importances_mean)

Gradient boosting Advanced

Boosting builds an ensemble in sequence, and each new model corrects the ensemble so far. Start with a constant $F_0(\mathbf x) = \bar y$. Then, for $m = 1, \dots, M$:

  1. Compute the pseudo-residuals, the negative gradient of the loss at the current predictions: $r_i = -\partial L(y_i, F)/\partial F\,\big|_{F = F_{m-1}(\mathbf x_i)}$.
  2. Fit a small regression tree $h_m$ to the pairs $(\mathbf x_i, r_i)$.
  3. Update $F_m(\mathbf x) = F_{m-1}(\mathbf x) + \nu\,h_m(\mathbf x)$.

For squared loss $L = \frac12(y - F)^2$ the pseudo-residual is the ordinary residual $y_i - F(\mathbf x_i)$, so each tree fits what the ensemble still gets wrong. For log loss it becomes $y_i - p_i$. The whole method is gradient descent in function space, one tree per step. The learning rate $\nu \in (0, 1]$ (shrinkage) scales each step: a smaller $\nu$ needs more trees and generalizes better. The trees stay shallow (depth 3 to 8), so each is a weak learner with high bias. Boosting reduces bias; bagging reduces variance.

AdaBoost, the older variant, reweights the training points instead. After round $t$ with weighted error $\varepsilon_t$, it gives the new learner the vote $\alpha_t = \frac12\ln\frac{1 - \varepsilon_t}{\varepsilon_t}$ and multiplies the weight of each misclassified point by $e^{\alpha_t}$. It matches gradient boosting with the exponential loss $e^{-yF}$.

Analogy. Golf: the first tree is the drive toward the hole, and each next tree is a putt that corrects the distance left. The learning rate says how hard you hit each putt.

Press "Add tree". The gray segments are the current residuals, the orange steps show the next tree fit to them, and the blue curve is the ensemble. Large $\nu$ fits fast and chases noise; small $\nu$ needs more trees.

From scratch Python · NumPy + scikit-learn tree
import numpy as np
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeRegressor

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x = np.sort(rng.uniform(0, 6.28, 200))[:, None]
y = np.sin(x[:, 0]) + 0.3 * rng.standard_normal(200)
nu, M = 0.1, 200
F = np.full_like(y, y.mean())                       # F0 = mean
trees = []
for m in range(M):
    r = y - F                                       # pseudo-residuals for squared loss
    h = DecisionTreeRegressor(max_depth=2).fit(x, r)
    F += nu * h.predict(x)                          # F_m = F_{m-1} + ν h_m
    trees.append(h)
predict = lambda xq: y.mean() + nu * sum(t.predict(xq) for t in trees)
print("train MSE", ((y - F) ** 2).mean(), predict(np.array([[1.57]])))
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.ensemble import GradientBoostingRegressor, HistGradientBoostingRegressor

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.uniform(0, 6.28, (500, 1))
y = np.sin(X[:, 0]) + 0.3 * rng.standard_normal(500)
gb = GradientBoostingRegressor(n_estimators=200, learning_rate=0.1, max_depth=2, subsample=0.8).fit(X[:400], y[:400])
print(gb.score(X[400:], y[400:]))
hgb = HistGradientBoostingRegressor(learning_rate=0.1, max_iter=300, early_stopping=True).fit(X[:400], y[:400])  # fast, binned
print(hgb.score(X[400:], y[400:]), hgb.n_iter_)

XGBoost Advanced

XGBoost (extreme gradient boosting) adds three things to gradient boosting: a second-order view of the loss, an explicit penalty on tree complexity, and engineering for speed. At round $t$ the ensemble predicts $\hat y_i^{(t-1)}$ and you add a tree $f_t$. Expand the loss to second order () around the current prediction, with gradients $g_i = \partial_{\hat y}L(y_i, \hat y_i^{(t-1)})$ and Hessians $h_i = \partial^2_{\hat y}L(y_i, \hat y_i^{(t-1)})$:

$$\mathcal L^{(t)} \approx \sum_i\Big[g_i f_t(\mathbf x_i) + \tfrac12 h_i f_t(\mathbf x_i)^2\Big] + \Omega(f_t), \qquad \Omega(f) = \gamma T + \tfrac12\lambda\sum_{j=1}^T w_j^2$$

$T$ counts the leaves and $w_j$ is the prediction of leaf $j$. Each point lands in one leaf, so with $G_j = \sum_{i \in j} g_i$ and $H_j = \sum_{i\in j} h_i$ the objective splits into one quadratic per leaf. Minimize each one:

$$w_j^* = -\frac{G_j}{H_j + \lambda}, \qquad \mathcal L^* = -\frac12\sum_{j=1}^T\frac{G_j^2}{H_j + \lambda} + \gamma T$$

The leaf weight is a Newton step () with L2 shrinkage. The same formula scores a candidate split of a leaf into left and right children:

$$\text{Gain} = \frac12\left[\frac{G_L^2}{H_L + \lambda} + \frac{G_R^2}{H_R + \lambda} - \frac{(G_L + G_R)^2}{H_L + H_R + \lambda}\right] - \gamma$$

A split happens only if its gain is positive, so $\gamma$ works as a minimum gain and prunes the tree. For squared loss, $g_i = \hat y_i - y_i$ and $h_i = 1$, so $H_j$ counts the points in the leaf. For log loss, $g_i = p_i - y_i$ and $h_i = p_i(1 - p_i)$: points the model already predicts with confidence get a small $h_i$ and count for less.

XGBoost is also fast. It groups each feature's values into bins, so it tests fewer thresholds, and it scans features in parallel. At each split it learns which way to send missing values, and it trains each tree on a random subset of rows and columns. LightGBM grows trees leaf-wise over histogram bins and samples rows by gradient size. CatBoost uses ordered boosting and symmetric trees to handle categorical features.

Analogy. XGBoost putts with a rangefinder: it knows the distance (the gradient) and how the green curves (the Hessian), so it sets the strength of each putt with a Newton step. It also pays a fee for each extra leaf ($\gamma$), which stops it from splitting for tiny gains.

One boosting round on 1D data, starting from a constant prediction. Drag the split (or press "Best split"). The readout computes $G$, $H$, the leaf weights and the gain; the lower plot shows the gain at each threshold. Raise $\lambda$ to shrink the leaf weights, raise $\gamma$ until no split pays off.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def split_gain(x, g, h, lam=1.0, gamma=0.0):
    """Best threshold on one feature by XGBoost's gain formula."""
    order = np.argsort(x)
    x, g, h = x[order], g[order], h[order]
    G, H = g.sum(), h.sum()
    GL, HL = np.cumsum(g)[:-1], np.cumsum(h)[:-1]
    GR, HR = G - GL, H - HL
    gain = 0.5 * (GL ** 2 / (HL + lam) + GR ** 2 / (HR + lam) - G ** 2 / (H + lam)) - gamma
    i = gain.argmax()
    t = (x[i] + x[i + 1]) / 2
    w_left, w_right = -GL[i] / (HL[i] + lam), -GR[i] / (HR[i] + lam)   # Newton step per leaf
    return float(t), float(gain[i]), float(w_left), float(w_right)

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x = rng.uniform(0, 10, 30)
y = 1 + 2.4 * (x > 6) + 0.7 * (x > 3) + 0.45 * rng.standard_normal(30)
pred = np.full_like(y, y.mean())
g, h = pred - y, np.ones_like(y)                    # squared loss: g = ŷ − y, h = 1
print(split_gain(x, g, h))
yb = (x + 1.6 * rng.standard_normal(30) > 5.5).astype(float)
p = np.full_like(yb, 0.5)                           # log loss: g = p − y, h = p(1 − p)
print(split_gain(x, p - yb, p * (1 - p)))
Ready to use Python · XGBoost
import numpy as np
import xgboost as xgb

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.normal(size=(1000, 5))
y = (X[:, 0] + X[:, 1] ** 2 - X[:, 2] + 0.3 * rng.standard_normal(1000) > 1).astype(int)
model = xgb.XGBClassifier(n_estimators=300, learning_rate=0.1, max_depth=4, reg_lambda=1.0, gamma=0.0,
                          subsample=0.8, colsample_bytree=0.8, early_stopping_rounds=20, eval_metric="logloss")
model.fit(X[:800], y[:800], eval_set=[(X[800:], y[800:])], verbose=False)
print(model.best_iteration, (model.predict(X[800:]) == y[800:]).mean())
print(model.feature_importances_.round(3))

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

Why does a random forest pick a random subset of features at each split?

With bootstrap samples alone, the strongest features dominate the top of each tree and the trees come out correlated, and averaging correlated trees removes little variance. Random feature subsets decorrelate the trees.

What does each new tree in gradient boosting fit?

The negative gradient of the loss with respect to the current predictions, which for squared error is the residual. The ensemble adds the new tree scaled by a small learning rate.

Unsupervised learning

k-means clustering Beginner

k-means splits unlabeled points into $k$ clusters. Each cluster has a center $\boldsymbol\mu_j$, and the algorithm minimizes the total squared distance from each point to its own center:

$$J = \sum_{i=1}^n \big\|\mathbf x_i - \boldsymbol\mu_{c(i)}\big\|^2$$

Lloyd's algorithm alternates two steps, and neither step can raise $J$:

  1. Assign: send each point to its nearest center, $c(i) = \arg\min_j\|\mathbf x_i - \boldsymbol\mu_j\|^2$.
  2. Update: move each center to the mean of its points, $\boldsymbol\mu_j = \frac{1}{|S_j|}\sum_{i \in S_j}\mathbf x_i$.

Repeat until the assignments stop changing. $J$ only goes down and can take only a finite number of values, so the loop ends, but at a local minimum that depends on the start. k-means++ picks the first center at random and each next center with probability proportional to its squared distance from the nearest chosen center. It spreads the starting centers out and comes with an $O(\log k)$ approximation guarantee.

To choose $k$, plot $J$ against $k$ and look for the elbow, or use the silhouette score. k-means assumes round clusters of similar size. Elongated or nested clusters need GMMs () or density-based methods such as DBSCAN.

Analogy. Open $k$ pizza shops in a city. Each customer walks to the nearest shop; then each shop moves to the middle of its customers. Repeat until nobody switches shops.

Press Step to alternate assign and update; the background shows which center owns each region. Try a random start several times, then compare with k-means++.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def kmeans(X, k, iters=100, seed=0):
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    C = [X[rng.integers(len(X))]]                    # k-means++ initialization
    for _ in range(k - 1):
        d2 = np.min(((X[:, None] - np.array(C)[None]) ** 2).sum(-1), axis=1)
        C.append(X[rng.choice(len(X), p=d2 / d2.sum())])
    C = np.array(C)
    for _ in range(iters):
        labels = ((X[:, None] - C[None]) ** 2).sum(-1).argmin(1)             # assign
        new = np.array([X[labels == j].mean(0) if (labels == j).any() else C[j] for j in range(k)])  # update
        if np.allclose(new, C):
            break
        C = new
    J = ((X - C[labels]) ** 2).sum()
    return C, labels, J

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal(c, 0.3, (50, 2)) for c in ([-1, 1], [1, 1], [0, -1])])
C, labels, J = kmeans(X, 3)
print(C.round(2), J)
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.cluster import KMeans
from sklearn.metrics import silhouette_score

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = np.vstack([rng.normal(c, 0.3, (50, 2)) for c in ([-1, 1], [1, 1], [0, -1])])
for k in (2, 3, 4, 5):
    km = KMeans(n_clusters=k, n_init="auto", random_state=0).fit(X)
    print(k, round(km.inertia_, 1), round(silhouette_score(X, km.labels_), 3))   # elbow and silhouette

Principal component analysis Intermediate

PCA finds the directions along which the data varies most. Center the data and compute the covariance matrix $S = \frac1n X^\top X$. The first principal component is the unit vector $\mathbf w$ that maximizes the variance of the projections $\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x$:

$$\max_{\mathbf w}\ \mathbf w^\top S\,\mathbf w \quad\text{subject to}\quad \|\mathbf w\| = 1$$

A Lagrange multiplier () turns the condition into $S\mathbf w = \lambda\mathbf w$. The components are eigenvectors of $S$ (), and the variance along each equals its eigenvalue. Sort the eigenvalues, keep the top $k$ eigenvectors as the columns of $W_k$, and project: $\mathbf z = W_k^\top\mathbf x$.

Maximizing the projected variance gives the same answer as minimizing the reconstruction error $\sum_i\|\mathbf x_i - W_kW_k^\top\mathbf x_i\|^2$. The reason is Pythagoras: for each point, the two squared lengths add up to its fixed squared distance from the mean. In practice you compute PCA from the SVD of the centered data, $X = U\Sigma V^\top$ (). The principal directions are the columns of $V$, the eigenvalues are $\sigma_i^2/n$, and the explained variance ratio $\lambda_j/\sum_i\lambda_i$ tells you how much each component keeps. Standardize first if the features have different units ().

Analogy. Photograph a 3D object from the angle that shows the most detail. PCA picks the camera angle where the data spreads out most, so the flat picture loses the least.

Drag the handle to rotate the projection line. Green bars show projected variance, red shows reconstruction error; they always add to the same total. The variance peaks, and the error bottoms out, on the first principal component.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
Z = rng.standard_normal((200, 2))
X = Z @ np.array([[1.1, 0.75], [0.0, 0.4]])         # correlated data
Xc = X - X.mean(0)
U, s, Vt = np.linalg.svd(Xc, full_matrices=False)
components = Vt                                      # rows: principal directions
explained = s ** 2 / (len(X) - 1)                   # eigenvalues of the covariance
print(components, explained / explained.sum())
scores = Xc @ Vt[:1].T                               # project onto PC1
recon = scores @ Vt[:1] + X.mean(0)
print("reconstruction MSE", ((X - recon) ** 2).sum(1).mean())
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
X = rng.standard_normal((200, 2)) @ np.array([[1.1, 0.75], [0.0, 0.4]])
pca = PCA(n_components=2).fit(X)
print(pca.components_, pca.explained_variance_ratio_)
print(PCA(n_components=0.95).fit(X).n_components_)    # keep enough components for 95% of the variance

Gaussian mixtures and EM Advanced

A Gaussian mixture model says each point comes from one of $K$ Gaussians, picked with probabilities $\pi_k$:

$$p(\mathbf x) = \sum_{k=1}^K \pi_k\,\mathcal N(\mathbf x \mid \boldsymbol\mu_k, \Sigma_k)$$

You don't observe which component produced each point, so maximum likelihood has no closed form. Expectation–maximization (EM) alternates two steps. The E-step computes each point's responsibilities, the posterior probability that component $k$ produced it:

$$r_{ik} = \frac{\pi_k\,\mathcal N(\mathbf x_i \mid \boldsymbol\mu_k, \Sigma_k)}{\sum_j \pi_j\,\mathcal N(\mathbf x_i \mid \boldsymbol\mu_j, \Sigma_j)}$$

The M-step refits each Gaussian to all points, weighted by responsibility. With $N_k = \sum_i r_{ik}$:

$$\pi_k = \frac{N_k}{n}, \qquad \boldsymbol\mu_k = \frac{1}{N_k}\sum_i r_{ik}\mathbf x_i, \qquad \Sigma_k = \frac{1}{N_k}\sum_i r_{ik}(\mathbf x_i - \boldsymbol\mu_k)(\mathbf x_i - \boldsymbol\mu_k)^\top$$

No iteration lowers the log-likelihood. EM climbs a lower bound on it, the ELBO, the same quantity a VAE maximizes (). k-means is the limit of EM with equal spherical covariances shrinking to zero: the responsibilities harden into 0/1 assignments. A GMM gives you soft clusters, elliptical shapes, and a density you can sample from or use to score anomalies.

Analogy. A person can belong 70% to the runners' club and 30% to the cyclists' club. The E-step guesses these memberships; the M-step redraws each club's profile from its weighted members.

Each EM step recolors the points by responsibility (mixed colors mean shared points) and refits the ellipses. The log-likelihood rises with each step.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def log_gauss(X, mu, S):
    d = X.shape[1]
    diff = X - mu
    sol = np.linalg.solve(S, diff.T).T
    return -0.5 * (d * np.log(2 * np.pi) + np.linalg.slogdet(S)[1] + (diff * sol).sum(1))

def em_gmm(X, K, iters=100, seed=0):
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    n, d = X.shape
    mu = X[rng.choice(n, K, replace=False)]
    S = np.array([np.cov(X.T) for _ in range(K)])
    pi = np.full(K, 1 / K)
    for _ in range(iters):
        logp = np.column_stack([np.log(pi[k]) + log_gauss(X, mu[k], S[k]) for k in range(K)])
        ll = np.logaddexp.reduce(logp, axis=1)
        R = np.exp(logp - ll[:, None])                       # E-step: responsibilities
        Nk = R.sum(0)                                        # M-step below
        pi, mu = Nk / n, (R.T @ X) / Nk[:, None]
        S = np.array([((R[:, k, None] * (X - mu[k])).T @ (X - mu[k])) / Nk[k] + 1e-6 * np.eye(d) for k in range(K)])
    return pi, mu, S, ll.sum()

rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
X = np.vstack([rng.multivariate_normal(m, c, 150) for m, c in (([-1, 0], [[0.3, 0.2], [0.2, 0.3]]), ([1.5, 1], [[0.1, 0], [0, 0.5]]))])
pi, mu, S, ll = em_gmm(X, 2)
print(pi.round(3), mu.round(2), round(ll, 2))
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.mixture import GaussianMixture

rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
X = np.vstack([rng.multivariate_normal(m, c, 150) for m, c in (([-1, 0], [[0.3, 0.2], [0.2, 0.3]]), ([1.5, 1], [[0.1, 0], [0, 0.5]]))])
for K in (1, 2, 3):
    gm = GaussianMixture(n_components=K, covariance_type="full", random_state=0).fit(X)
    print(K, round(gm.bic(X), 1))                 # lower BIC = better trade-off of fit and size
gm = GaussianMixture(2, random_state=0).fit(X)
print(gm.weights_.round(3), gm.means_.round(2), gm.predict_proba(X[:2]).round(3))

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

k-means lowers its objective at each step, yet it can end in a poor clustering. Why, and what is the usual fix?

Each step lowers the within-cluster sum of squares, so the algorithm converges, but to a local minimum that depends on the start. Use k-means++ starts and keep the best of several runs.

Which direction is the first principal component of centered data?

The eigenvector of the covariance matrix with the largest eigenvalue, which is the top right singular vector of the data matrix. Its eigenvalue is the variance of the data along that direction.

How do Gaussian mixtures assign points, compared with k-means?

A mixture gives each point a probability (responsibility) for each component, and each component has its own covariance, so a cluster can stretch or tilt. k-means makes hard assignments to round clusters.

Probabilistic models

Gaussian processes Expert

A Gaussian process (GP) is a probability distribution over functions. Any finite set of values $f(x_1), \dots, f(x_n)$ is jointly Gaussian, with a covariance that a kernel supplies:

$$f \sim \mathcal{GP}\big(0,\ k(x, x')\big), \qquad k(x, x') = \sigma_f^2\exp\Big(-\frac{(x - x')^2}{2\ell^2}\Big)$$

The length scale $\ell$ says how far apart two inputs can sit and still have correlated outputs, and $\sigma_f^2$ sets the vertical scale. Observe noisy targets $\mathbf y = f(X) + \boldsymbol\varepsilon$ with $\boldsymbol\varepsilon \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, \sigma_n^2 I)$. Conditioning a Gaussian on some of its values gives another Gaussian, so the prediction at a new input $x_*$ has a closed form:

$$\mu_* = \mathbf k_*^\top (K + \sigma_n^2 I)^{-1}\mathbf y, \qquad \sigma_*^2 = k(x_*, x_*) - \mathbf k_*^\top(K + \sigma_n^2 I)^{-1}\mathbf k_*$$

Here $K_{ij} = k(x_i, x_j)$ and $(\mathbf k_*)_i = k(x_i, x_*)$. The mean passes near the data. The variance shrinks near observations and grows back to $\sigma_f^2$ far from them. The inverse costs $O(n^3)$ (use a Cholesky factorization), which limits exact GPs to about $10^4$ points; sparse and inducing-point methods go further. You fit $\ell$, $\sigma_f$ and $\sigma_n$ by maximizing the log marginal likelihood

$$\log p(\mathbf y) = -\tfrac12\mathbf y^\top(K + \sigma_n^2 I)^{-1}\mathbf y - \tfrac12\log\big|K + \sigma_n^2 I\big| - \tfrac n2\log 2\pi,$$

whose first term rewards fit and whose second term penalizes complexity.

Analogy. A GP draws a curve through your measurements with a confidence ribbon around it: tight near the measurements and wide far from them, like a weather forecast that is sure about tomorrow and vague about next month.

Click to add observations. The blue line is the posterior mean, the band is ±2 standard deviations, and the thin lines are functions sampled from the posterior. Shrink $\ell$ and the samples wiggle; raise $\sigma_n$ and the mean stops passing through the points.

In ML: Bayesian optimization tunes hyperparameters with a GP surrogate, placing the next trial where the mean looks good or the uncertainty is high. Wide neural networks at initialization behave like GPs (the neural tangent kernel).

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def rbf(A, B, ell=1.0, sf=1.0):
    d2 = (A[:, None] - B[None]) ** 2
    return sf ** 2 * np.exp(-d2 / (2 * ell ** 2))

X = np.array([-3.0, -1.5, 0.5, 2.0])
y = np.array([-0.5, 0.8, 0.3, -1.1])
Xs = np.linspace(-5, 5, 9)
sn = 0.1
K = rbf(X, X) + sn ** 2 * np.eye(len(X))
L = np.linalg.cholesky(K)
alpha = np.linalg.solve(L.T, np.linalg.solve(L, y))     # (K + σ²I)⁻¹ y
Ks = rbf(X, Xs)
mean = Ks.T @ alpha
v = np.linalg.solve(L, Ks)
std = np.sqrt(np.diag(rbf(Xs, Xs)) - (v ** 2).sum(0))
lml = -0.5 * y @ alpha - np.log(np.diag(L)).sum() - len(X) / 2 * np.log(2 * np.pi)
print(mean.round(3), std.round(3), round(lml, 3))
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn
import numpy as np
from sklearn.gaussian_process import GaussianProcessRegressor
from sklearn.gaussian_process.kernels import RBF, ConstantKernel, WhiteKernel

X = np.array([[-3.0], [-1.5], [0.5], [2.0], [3.5], [-4.0]])
y = np.sin(X[:, 0])
kernel = ConstantKernel(1.0) * RBF(length_scale=1.0) + WhiteKernel(1e-2)
gp = GaussianProcessRegressor(kernel=kernel, normalize_y=True, random_state=0).fit(X, y)  # fits ℓ, σ_f, σ_n by max marginal likelihood
mean, std = gp.predict(np.linspace(-5, 5, 5)[:, None], return_std=True)
print(gp.kernel_)
print(mean.round(3), std.round(3), round(gp.log_marginal_likelihood_value_, 3))

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

What does a Gaussian process return at a test point besides a prediction?

A variance. The prediction is a full Gaussian, and its variance grows away from the training data, so the model reports where it is unsure.

What limits exact Gaussian processes to small datasets?

Inverting the $n\times n$ kernel matrix costs $O(n^3)$ time and $O(n^2)$ memory, which caps exact GPs at about $10^4$ points. Sparse and inducing-point methods go further.
