  • ultralytics/ultralytics: the library behind the YOLO object detectors. Project: label 100 to 200 photos of objects you care about (motorbikes on the road, fruit at a market), train a YOLO detector on them, and read its precision, recall and mAP. Practices , , .
  • milesial/Pytorch-UNet: a clean U-Net for semantic segmentation, with the Dice score in utils/dice_score.py. Project: train it on the Carvana car-masking data the repo uses, then swap in your own images and masks. Practices , .

Part VI. Generative models

A generative model learns the data distribution $p(\mathbf x)$ well enough to draw new samples from it. The four families below differ in how they get around the fact that $p(\mathbf x)$ is intractable for images and text.

Generative models

Variational autoencoders Expert

An autoencoder compresses an input to a short code $\mathbf z = f_{\text{enc}}(\mathbf x)$ and reconstructs it, $\hat{\mathbf x} = f_{\text{dec}}(\mathbf z)$, trained to minimize $\|\mathbf x - \hat{\mathbf x}\|^2$. Its code space has holes: decode a random $\mathbf z$ and you get garbage. A variational autoencoder (VAE) makes the code a distribution and pulls it toward a prior $p(\mathbf z) = \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, I)$, so you can sample new data by decoding draws from the prior.

The encoder outputs a mean and a standard deviation, $q_\phi(\mathbf z\mid\mathbf x) = \mathcal N\big(\boldsymbol\mu_\phi(\mathbf x), \operatorname{diag}\boldsymbol\sigma_\phi^2(\mathbf x)\big)$, and the decoder defines $p_\theta(\mathbf x\mid\mathbf z)$. The log-likelihood is intractable, but it has a lower bound, the evidence lower bound (ELBO):

$$\log p_\theta(\mathbf x) \;\ge\; \underbrace{\mathbb E_{q_\phi(\mathbf z\mid\mathbf x)}\big[\log p_\theta(\mathbf x\mid\mathbf z)\big]}_{\text{reconstruction}} \;-\; \underbrace{D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(q_\phi(\mathbf z\mid\mathbf x)\,\|\,p(\mathbf z)\big)}_{\text{stay near the prior}}$$

The gap equals $D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(q_\phi(\mathbf z\mid\mathbf x)\,\|\,p_\theta(\mathbf z\mid\mathbf x)\big)$, so maximizing the ELBO also makes the encoder approximate the true posterior. For a Gaussian encoder and prior, the KL term has a closed form ():

$$D_{\mathrm{KL}} = \frac12\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big(\mu_j^2 + \sigma_j^2 - 1 - \log\sigma_j^2\big)$$

You can't backpropagate through a random draw. The reparameterization trick moves the randomness into an input: draw $\boldsymbol\epsilon \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, I)$ and set $\mathbf z = \boldsymbol\mu + \boldsymbol\sigma \odot \boldsymbol\epsilon$. Now $\mathbf z$ is a differentiable function of $\boldsymbol\mu$ and $\boldsymbol\sigma$, and gradients flow. A β-VAE weights the KL term by $\beta$ to trade reconstruction quality against a more organized latent space.

Analogy. A VAE files each image as a fuzzy patch on a map, where a plain autoencoder would use a pin. The KL term keeps all the patches inside one shared region, so any point you pick there decodes to a plausible image.

Gray: the prior $\mathcal N(0, 1)$. Blue: the encoder's $q(z\mid x)$ for one input. The dots are samples $z = \mu + \sigma\epsilon$ with fixed $\epsilon$: move $\mu$ and $\sigma$ and the same dots slide without jumps, so gradients can pass through them. The KL penalty is zero only when $q$ matches the prior.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
mu, logvar = np.array([1.2, -0.5]), np.array([np.log(0.25), np.log(2.0)])   # encoder output for one input
sigma = np.exp(0.5 * logvar)
eps = rng.standard_normal((100_000, 2))
z = mu + sigma * eps                                     # reparameterization: z = μ + σ ⊙ ε
kl_closed = 0.5 * np.sum(mu ** 2 + sigma ** 2 - 1 - logvar)
log_q = -0.5 * (((z - mu) / sigma) ** 2 + logvar + np.log(2 * np.pi)).sum(1)
log_p = -0.5 * (z ** 2 + np.log(2 * np.pi)).sum(1)
print(kl_closed, (log_q - log_p).mean())                 # closed form = Monte Carlo estimate
Ready to use Python · PyTorch
import math
import torch
from torch import nn

class VAE(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_in=2, d_z=2, h=64):
        super().__init__()
        self.enc = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_in, h), nn.ReLU(), nn.Linear(h, 2 * d_z))
        self.dec = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_z, h), nn.ReLU(), nn.Linear(h, d_in))

    def forward(self, x):
        mu, logvar = self.enc(x).chunk(2, dim=-1)
        z = mu + torch.exp(0.5 * logvar) * torch.randn_like(mu)          # reparameterization
        kl = 0.5 * (mu ** 2 + logvar.exp() - 1 - logvar).sum(-1)
        recon = ((self.dec(z) - x) ** 2).sum(-1) / (2 * 0.1 ** 2)         # Gaussian decoder with σ = 0.1, up to constants
        return (recon + kl).mean()                                        # negative ELBO

torch.manual_seed(0)
a = torch.rand(2000) * 2 * math.pi
data = torch.stack([torch.cos(a), torch.sin(a)], 1) + 0.05 * torch.randn(2000, 2)   # points on a ring
vae = VAE()
opt = torch.optim.Adam(vae.parameters(), lr=1e-3)
for step in range(1500):
    loss = vae(data[torch.randint(0, 2000, (128,))])
    opt.zero_grad(); loss.backward(); opt.step()
samples = vae.dec(torch.randn(1000, 2)).detach()                          # decode samples from the prior
print(loss.item(), samples.norm(dim=1).mean().item(), "| ring radius: 1.0")

Generative adversarial networks Expert

A GAN trains two networks against each other. The generator $G$ turns noise $\mathbf z \sim p(\mathbf z)$ into samples $G(\mathbf z)$. The discriminator $D$ outputs the probability that its input is real. They play a minimax game:

$$\min_G\max_D\ \ \mathbb E_{\mathbf x\sim p_{\text{data}}}\big[\log D(\mathbf x)\big] + \mathbb E_{\mathbf z\sim p(\mathbf z)}\big[\log\big(1 - D(G(\mathbf z))\big)\big]$$

For a fixed generator, the best discriminator is $D^*(\mathbf x) = \frac{p_{\text{data}}(\mathbf x)}{p_{\text{data}}(\mathbf x) + p_g(\mathbf x)}$. Substitute it, and the generator minimizes $2\,\mathrm{JS}(p_{\text{data}}\,\|\,p_g) - \log 4$, where JS is the Jensen–Shannon divergence. The optimum is $p_g = p_{\text{data}}$, where $D^* = \frac12$ everywhere.

In practice the generator maximizes $\log D(G(\mathbf z))$ instead (the non-saturating loss): early in training $D$ rejects fakes with confidence, and the original gradient vanishes. Training is unstable. The generator may cover only part of the data (mode collapse), or the two players may oscillate. Remedies include Wasserstein GANs with a gradient penalty, spectral normalization, and tuned architectures (DCGAN, StyleGAN).

Analogy. A counterfeiter (G) and a detective (D) train together. The counterfeiter improves until the detective can only guess, and at that point the fake bills match the real ones.

A 1D GAN. Gray: the data density. Blue: the generator's output, a shifted and scaled Gaussian. Red: $D(x)$. Train and watch the generator move onto the data while $D$ flattens toward 0.5. With two data bumps, this one-Gaussian generator can't cover both.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
sig = lambda z: 1 / (1 + np.exp(-z))
a, b = 0.4, -2.5                                  # generator G(z) = a z + b
w = np.zeros(3)                                   # discriminator D(x) = σ(w0 + w1 u + w2 u²), u = x/3
D = lambda x: sig(w[0] + w[1] * x / 3 + w[2] * (x / 3) ** 2)
feat = lambda x: np.stack([np.ones_like(x), x / 3, (x / 3) ** 2], 1)
for step in range(3000):
    for _ in range(5):                            # several discriminator steps per generator step
        real, fake = rng.normal(2.0, 0.6, 64), a * rng.standard_normal(64) + b
        grad_w = feat(real).T @ (D(real) - 1) + feat(fake).T @ D(fake)      # ∇ of −log D(real) − log(1 − D(fake))
        w -= 0.5 * grad_w / 64
    z = rng.standard_normal(64)
    x = a * z + b
    dx = -(1 - D(x)) * (w[1] + 2 * w[2] * x / 3) / 3                       # non-saturating loss −log D(G(z))
    a, b = max(0.05, a - 0.03 * (dx * z).mean()), b - 0.03 * dx.mean()
print("generator mean, std:", b, a, "| data: 2.0, 0.6")
Ready to use Python · PyTorch
import torch
from torch import nn

torch.manual_seed(0)
G = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(1, 32), nn.ReLU(), nn.Linear(32, 1))
D = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(1, 32), nn.LeakyReLU(0.2), nn.Linear(32, 1))
# TTUR: the discriminator learns faster than the generator; betas=(0.5, 0.999) is the usual GAN setting.
opt_g = torch.optim.Adam(G.parameters(), 1e-4, betas=(0.5, 0.999))
opt_d = torch.optim.Adam(D.parameters(), 4e-4, betas=(0.5, 0.999))
bce = nn.BCEWithLogitsLoss()
ones, zeros = torch.ones(256, 1), torch.zeros(256, 1)
for step in range(6000):
    real = 2.0 + 0.6 * torch.randn(256, 1)
    fake = G(torch.randn(256, 1))
    loss_d = bce(D(real), ones) + bce(D(fake.detach()), zeros)   # detach: don't update G here
    opt_d.zero_grad(); loss_d.backward(); opt_d.step()
    loss_g = bce(D(fake), ones)                                    # non-saturating generator loss
    opt_g.zero_grad(); loss_g.backward(); opt_g.step()
samples = G(torch.randn(5000, 1)).detach()
print(samples.mean().item(), samples.std().item(), "| data: 2.0, 0.6")   # the spread still wobbles between seeds: GANs are unstable

Diffusion models Expert

A diffusion model learns to reverse a gradual noising process. The forward process adds a little Gaussian noise at each of $T$ steps, a Markov chain ():

$$q(\mathbf x_t\mid\mathbf x_{t-1}) = \mathcal N\big(\sqrt{1 - \beta_t}\,\mathbf x_{t-1},\ \beta_t I\big)$$

With $\alpha_t = 1 - \beta_t$ and $\bar\alpha_t = \prod_{s\le t}\alpha_s$, you can jump to any step in closed form, and by $t = T$ the data has turned into pure noise:

$$\mathbf x_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,\mathbf x_0 + \sqrt{1 - \bar\alpha_t}\,\boldsymbol\epsilon, \qquad \boldsymbol\epsilon \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, I)$$

A network $\boldsymbol\epsilon_\theta(\mathbf x_t, t)$ learns to predict the added noise. The DDPM training loss is a plain regression:

$$L = \mathbb E_{t,\,\mathbf x_0,\,\boldsymbol\epsilon}\Big[\big\|\boldsymbol\epsilon - \boldsymbol\epsilon_\theta\big(\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,\mathbf x_0 + \sqrt{1 - \bar\alpha_t}\,\boldsymbol\epsilon,\ t\big)\big\|^2\Big]$$

To generate, start from noise $\mathbf x_T \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, I)$ and denoise step by step:

$$\mathbf x_{t-1} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_t}}\Big(\mathbf x_t - \frac{\beta_t}{\sqrt{1 - \bar\alpha_t}}\,\boldsymbol\epsilon_\theta(\mathbf x_t, t)\Big) + \sigma_t\mathbf z, \qquad \mathbf z \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, I)$$

Predicting the noise amounts to estimating the score $\nabla_{\mathbf x}\log p_t(\mathbf x) \approx -\boldsymbol\epsilon_\theta/\sqrt{1 - \bar\alpha_t}$, the direction toward higher data density. DDIM takes larger deterministic steps and samples in 20 to 50 steps instead of 1,000. Latent diffusion (Stable Diffusion) runs the process in a VAE's latent space. Classifier-free guidance strengthens the conditioning $c$ by extrapolating: $\hat{\boldsymbol\epsilon} = (1 + w)\,\boldsymbol\epsilon_\theta(\mathbf x_t, c) - w\,\boldsymbol\epsilon_\theta(\mathbf x_t)$.

Analogy. An ink drop spreads in a glass of water until the water looks a uniform gray: that is the forward process. The model learns to play the video backward, one small step at a time, turning uniform noise into a picture.

Slide $t$ to run the forward process on a spiral of points: the data dissolves into noise. Press Generate to run the reverse process (DDIM) from fresh noise. For this small dataset the figure uses the exact optimal denoiser in place of a trained network, so the samples land on the spiral.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

T = 100
s = 0.008
f = np.cos((np.arange(T + 1) / T + s) / (1 + s) * np.pi / 2) ** 2   # cosine schedule
beta = np.r_[0.0, np.clip(1 - f[1:] / f[:-1], 0, 0.999)]        # clip β first, then derive α and ᾱ from it
alpha = 1 - beta
alpha_bar = np.cumprod(alpha)

def q_sample(x0, t, rng):                                 # x_t = √ᾱ_t x0 + √(1−ᾱ_t) ε
    eps = rng.standard_normal(x0.shape)
    return np.sqrt(alpha_bar[t]) * x0 + np.sqrt(1 - alpha_bar[t]) * eps, eps

# Data ~ N(0, s_data²): the best noise predictor has a closed form, so we can test the sampler.
s_data = 0.5
eps_hat = lambda x, t: np.sqrt(1 - alpha_bar[t]) * x / (alpha_bar[t] * s_data ** 2 + 1 - alpha_bar[t])
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x = rng.standard_normal(20_000)                           # x_T: pure noise
for t in range(T, 0, -1):                                 # DDPM reverse step
    mean = (x - beta[t] / np.sqrt(1 - alpha_bar[t]) * eps_hat(x, t)) / np.sqrt(alpha[t])
    x = mean + (np.sqrt(beta[t]) * rng.standard_normal(x.shape) if t > 1 else 0)
print("sample std:", x.std(), "target:", s_data)
xt, eps = q_sample(rng.normal(0, s_data, 5), 50, rng)
print("training target is eps; loss = mean((eps - eps_model(x_t, t))**2)")
Ready to use Python · diffusers
import torch
from diffusers import DDPMScheduler, UNet2DModel

model = UNet2DModel(sample_size=16, in_channels=1, out_channels=1, layers_per_block=1, block_out_channels=(32, 64),
                    down_block_types=("DownBlock2D", "DownBlock2D"), up_block_types=("UpBlock2D", "UpBlock2D"))
sched = DDPMScheduler(num_train_timesteps=1000, beta_schedule="squaredcos_cap_v2")
opt = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=1e-4)
x0 = torch.randn(8, 1, 16, 16)                            # your training images, scaled to [-1, 1]
t = torch.randint(0, 1000, (8,))
noise = torch.randn_like(x0)
loss = torch.nn.functional.mse_loss(model(sched.add_noise(x0, noise, t), t).sample, noise)   # predict the noise
loss.backward(); opt.step()
print(loss.item())
sched.set_timesteps(20)                                   # sample with 20 steps
x = torch.randn(1, 1, 16, 16)
with torch.no_grad():
    for step in sched.timesteps:
        x = sched.step(model(x, step).sample, step, x).prev_sample
print(x.shape)

Flow matching Expert

Diffusion () generates by undoing a noising process step by step. Flow matching (Lipman and others, 2022), which Liu and others call rectified flow, learns a velocity field $\mathbf v_\theta(\mathbf x, t)$ and generates by following it. Start from noise $\mathbf x_0 \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, I)$ at $t = 0$ and solve the differential equation $d\mathbf x/dt = \mathbf v_\theta(\mathbf x, t)$ up to $t = 1$, where the samples look like data.

Training needs no simulation. Pair a noise sample $\mathbf x_0$ with a data sample $\mathbf x_1$, join them with a straight line, and teach the network the velocity of that line:

$$\mathbf x_t = (1 - t)\,\mathbf x_0 + t\,\mathbf x_1, \qquad L(\theta) = \mathbb E_{t,\,\mathbf x_0,\,\mathbf x_1}\big\|\mathbf v_\theta(\mathbf x_t, t) - (\mathbf x_1 - \mathbf x_0)\big\|^2$$

A point $\mathbf x_t$ lies on many such lines, so the network can't know which line it is on. The squared loss makes it predict the average direction, $\mathbf v(\mathbf x, t) = \mathbb E[\mathbf x_1 - \mathbf x_0 \mid \mathbf x_t = \mathbf x]$, and following that average field carries the whole noise distribution onto the data distribution. To generate, take $K$ Euler steps, $\mathbf x \leftarrow \mathbf x + \mathbf v_\theta(\mathbf x, t)/K$.

The averaged paths bend, so one big step fails. At $t = 0$ the average direction points at the mean of the data, and a single step lands each sample there. A few steps fix it, and reflow retrains on the model's own (noise, sample) pairs to straighten the paths further. Stable Diffusion 3 and FLUX generate images this way with a transformer as $\mathbf v_\theta$, and robot policies such as $\pi_0$ use it to generate motor actions.

Analogy. Pouring sand into a mold: each grain starts at a random spot and has to end somewhere in the shape. Flow matching learns a wind map, a direction to blow at each place and time, so that each grain carried by the wind lands in the shape. Training shows the wind straight trips from random spots to random points of the shape, and the wind learns their average.

Gray dots: noise samples at $t = 0$. Orange: the data, two interleaved moons. Blue: each sample at time $t$ after following the exact average velocity of this data with $K$ Euler steps; thin lines trace the paths. With $K = 1$ each sample lands on the mean of the data, and a handful of steps is enough.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
theta = rng.uniform(0, np.pi, 200)                     # data: two interleaved half circles ("moons")
data = np.r_[np.c_[np.cos(theta), np.sin(theta)], np.c_[1 - np.cos(theta), 0.5 - np.sin(theta)]] - [0.5, 0.25]

def velocity(x, t):
    """Exact marginal velocity E[x1 - x0 | x_t = x] for x_t = (1 - t) x0 + t x1, x0 ~ N(0, I), x1 ~ data."""
    d2 = ((x[:, None, :] - t * data[None]) ** 2).sum(-1)        # x_t | x1 is Gaussian around t·x1, sd (1 - t)
    w = np.exp(-(d2 - d2.min(1, keepdims=True)) / (2 * (1 - t) ** 2))
    w /= w.sum(1, keepdims=True)                                 # posterior weight of each data point
    return (w[..., None] * (data[None] - x[:, None, :])).sum(1) / (1 - t)

def sample(n, steps):
    x = rng.normal(size=(n, 2))                                  # start from noise at t = 0
    for k in range(steps):
        x = x + velocity(x, k / steps) / steps                   # Euler step along dx/dt = v(x, t)
    return x

for steps in (1, 2, 4, 16):
    x = sample(500, steps)
    gap = np.sqrt(((x[:, None] - data[None]) ** 2).sum(-1)).min(1).mean()
    print(f"{steps:2d} steps: mean distance to the nearest data point {gap:.3f}")   # 1 step lands every sample at the data mean

def fm_loss(v_model, x1):
    """The training loss: regress the network's velocity onto x1 - x0 along straight paths."""
    x0, t = rng.normal(size=x1.shape), rng.uniform(size=(len(x1), 1))
    xt = (1 - t) * x0 + t * x1
    return ((v_model(xt, t) - (x1 - x0)) ** 2).sum(1).mean()

print("loss of the exact field:", fm_loss(lambda x, t: np.stack([velocity(xi[None], ti[0])[0] for xi, ti in zip(x, t)]), data[:64]))
Ready to use Python · PyTorch
import math
import torch
from torch import nn

torch.manual_seed(0)
def moons(n):
    t = torch.rand(n) * math.pi
    first = torch.rand(n) < 0.5
    x = torch.where(first, torch.cos(t), 1 - torch.cos(t))
    y = torch.where(first, torch.sin(t), 0.5 - torch.sin(t))
    return torch.stack([x - 0.5, y - 0.25], 1)

net = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(3, 256), nn.SiLU(), nn.Linear(256, 256), nn.SiLU(),
                    nn.Linear(256, 256), nn.SiLU(), nn.Linear(256, 2))          # v_θ(x, t): input (x, y, t)
opt = torch.optim.Adam(net.parameters(), lr=2e-3)
sched = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.CosineAnnealingLR(opt, 4000)
for step in range(4000):                                                       # about half a minute on a laptop CPU
    x1 = moons(512)
    x0, t = torch.randn_like(x1), torch.rand(512, 1)
    xt = (1 - t) * x0 + t * x1                                                 # a point on the straight path
    loss = ((net(torch.cat([xt, t], 1)) - (x1 - x0)) ** 2).sum(1).mean()       # regress onto the path's velocity
    opt.zero_grad(); loss.backward(); opt.step(); sched.step()

@torch.no_grad()
def sample(n, steps):
    x = torch.randn(n, 2)
    for k in range(steps):
        x = x + net(torch.cat([x, torch.full((n, 1), k / steps)], 1)) / steps  # Euler steps from t = 0 to 1
    return x

ref = moons(4000)
for steps in (1, 4, 16):
    d = torch.cdist(sample(2000, steps), ref).min(1).values
    print(f"{steps:2d} steps: {(d < 0.05).float().mean():.0%} of samples within 0.05 of the data")
# At scale: diffusers' FlowMatchEulerDiscreteScheduler runs this sampler for Stable Diffusion 3 and FLUX.

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

Why does a VAE use the reparameterization trick?

You can't backpropagate through a random draw. Writing $\mathbf z = \boldsymbol\mu + \boldsymbol\sigma\odot\boldsymbol\epsilon$ with $\boldsymbol\epsilon \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, I)$ moves the randomness into an input, so $\mathbf z$ is differentiable in $\boldsymbol\mu$ and $\boldsymbol\sigma$.

What is mode collapse in a GAN?

The generator covers part of the data, a few kinds of output that fool the discriminator, and ignores the rest of the distribution.

What does the DDPM network predict, and what loss trains it?

The noise $\boldsymbol\epsilon$ mixed into a clean sample at step $t$. The loss is the squared error $\|\boldsymbol\epsilon - \boldsymbol\epsilon_\theta(\mathbf x_t, t)\|^2$.

Why does one Euler step of flow matching send each sample to the mean of the data?

At $t = 0$ a noise sample says nothing about which data point it will reach, so the average velocity is $\mathbb E[\mathbf x_1] - \mathbf x_0$, and a step of size 1 lands on $\mathbb E[\mathbf x_1]$.

Projects to build

  • pytorch/examples: small, official PyTorch examples. Project: train the VAE in vae/ on MNIST, change its latent size to 2 and plot the latent space; then train the GAN in dcgan/ and watch for mode collapse. Practices , .
  • lucidrains/denoising-diffusion-pytorch: a compact implementation of diffusion models (DDPM). Project: train it on a folder of your own small images, such as 64 × 64 flowers or faces, sample new ones, then cut the number of sampling steps with its DDIM option. Practices .

Part VII. Reinforcement learning

In reinforcement learning an agent acts, the environment answers with a reward and a new state, and the agent learns a behavior that collects as much reward as possible over time. Nobody labels the right action; the agent finds it by trial and error.

Reinforcement learning

Exploration: multi-armed bandits Intermediate

A bandit is the simplest reinforcement learning problem: $K$ slot machines (arms), each paying a random reward with an unknown mean $\mu_a$. Each round you pull one arm. To earn the most, you must explore (try arms to learn their means) and exploit (pull the arm that looks best). You measure a strategy by its regret, the reward lost against always pulling the best arm:

$$R_T = T\mu^* - \sum_{t=1}^T \mu_{a_t}$$

ε-greedy pulls the arm with the best estimated mean $\hat\mu_a$, except that with probability ε it picks a random arm. It never stops exploring, so its regret grows linearly. UCB1 (upper confidence bound) pulls the arm with the best optimistic estimate:

$$a_t = \arg\max_a\Big[\hat\mu_a + \sqrt{\frac{2\ln t}{n_a}}\Big]$$

Here $n_a$ counts the pulls of arm $a$. The bonus is large for arms you have tried only a few times, and it shrinks as data comes in. UCB therefore explores where it is uncertain, and its regret grows like $\log T$. Thompson sampling draws a plausible mean for each arm from its posterior () and pulls the arm with the best draw.

Analogy. Pick a restaurant tonight: your favorite (exploit) or a new place that might be better (explore). UCB gives each place an optimism bonus that is large when you have eaten there only a few times.

Five arms with hidden payout rates. Three strategies play 1,000 rounds each. Left: cumulative regret (lower is better). ε-greedy's regret climbs in a straight line. UCB1's curve bends over, as $\log T$ predicts, but its generous bonus costs a lot up front; on arms this close it needs tens of thousands of rounds to pass ε-greedy. Thompson sampling wins at every horizon. Right: how often each strategy pulled each arm; the best arm is marked ★.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def play(policy, mus, T=1000, eps=0.1, seed=0):
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    K = len(mus)
    n, s = np.zeros(K), np.zeros(K)                    # pulls and total reward per arm
    regret = 0.0
    for t in range(1, T + 1):
        if t <= K:
            a = t - 1                                  # try each arm once
        elif policy == "eps-greedy":
            a = rng.integers(K) if rng.random() < eps else np.argmax(s / n)
        elif policy == "ucb1":
            a = np.argmax(s / n + np.sqrt(2 * np.log(t) / n))       # optimism bonus
        else:                                          # thompson: sample each arm's Beta posterior
            a = np.argmax(rng.beta(1 + s, 1 + n - s))
        r = float(rng.random() < mus[a])
        n[a] += 1; s[a] += r
        regret += max(mus) - mus[a]
    return regret, n.astype(int)

mus = [0.3, 0.5, 0.45, 0.7, 0.6]
for p in ("eps-greedy", "ucb1", "thompson"):
    print(p, play(p, mus))
Ready to use Python · MABWiser
import numpy as np
from mabwiser.mab import MAB, LearningPolicy

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
mus = {"A": 0.3, "B": 0.5, "C": 0.45, "D": 0.7, "E": 0.6}
mab = MAB(arms=list(mus), learning_policy=LearningPolicy.ThompsonSampling(), seed=0)
mab.fit(decisions=list(mus), rewards=[float(rng.random() < p) for p in mus.values()])   # one pull per arm to start
for _ in range(1000):
    arm = mab.predict()
    mab.partial_fit([arm], [float(rng.random() < mus[arm])])
print(mab.predict(), {arm: round(float(v), 3) for arm, v in mab.predict_expectations().items()})
# Other policies: LearningPolicy.EpsilonGreedy(epsilon=0.1), LearningPolicy.UCB1(alpha=1.0)

Markov decision processes and value iteration Advanced

A Markov decision process (MDP) adds actions and rewards to a Markov chain (). It has states $s$, actions $a$, transition probabilities $P(s'\mid s, a)$, rewards $R(s, a)$ and a discount factor $\gamma \in [0, 1)$. A policy $\pi(a\mid s)$ chooses actions, and the goal is to maximize the expected discounted return $G_t = \sum_{k=0}^\infty\gamma^k r_{t+k+1}$. The discount keeps the sum finite and favors sooner rewards.

The value of a state under policy $\pi$ is its expected return, $V^\pi(s) = \mathbb E_\pi[G_t\mid s_t = s]$, and the action value $Q^\pi(s, a)$ also fixes the first action. Values satisfy the Bellman equations, which split the return into the next reward plus the discounted value of the next state:

$$V^\pi(s) = \sum_a\pi(a\mid s)\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s, a)\big[R(s, a) + \gamma V^\pi(s')\big], \qquad V^*(s) = \max_a\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s, a)\big[R(s, a) + \gamma V^*(s')\big]$$

Value iteration turns the optimality equation into an update and sweeps it over all states until the values stop changing:

$$V_{k+1}(s) \leftarrow \max_a\sum_{s'}P(s'\mid s, a)\big[R(s, a) + \gamma V_k(s')\big]$$

The update shrinks the distance to $V^*$ by a factor $\gamma$ per sweep, so it converges from any start. The optimal policy picks, in each state, the action with the best value under $V^*$. Policy iteration alternates a full evaluation of the current policy with a greedy improvement and often needs fewer sweeps.

Analogy. Value spreads backward from the goal like water filling a valley: cells next to the goal fill first, then their neighbors, losing a little at each step (the discount).

A gridworld. Click a cell to cycle it through wall, +1 goal, −1 pit and empty. Each sweep updates every value; arrows show the greedy policy. With "slip" the agent sometimes moves sideways, so the policy learns to keep its distance from the pit.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

# 4x4 grid: start top-left, goal (+1) bottom-right, holes (-1). Moves can slip sideways.
grid = ["S..H", ".H..", "...H", "H..G"]
n, A = 16, [(-1, 0), (0, 1), (1, 0), (0, -1)]          # up, right, down, left
gamma, slip = 0.95, 0.2

def step(s, a):
    r, c = divmod(s, 4)
    r2, c2 = min(3, max(0, r + A[a][0])), min(3, max(0, c + A[a][1]))
    return r2 * 4 + c2

def reward(s):
    return {"G": 1.0, "H": -1.0}.get(grid[s // 4][s % 4], 0.0)

terminal = [grid[s // 4][s % 4] in "GH" for s in range(n)]
V = np.zeros(n)
for sweep in range(500):
    Q = np.zeros((n, 4))
    for s in range(n):
        if terminal[s]:
            continue
        for a in range(4):
            for b, p in ((a, 1 - slip), ((a + 1) % 4, slip / 2), ((a + 3) % 4, slip / 2)):
                s2 = step(s, b)
                Q[s, a] += p * (reward(s2) + gamma * (0 if terminal[s2] else V[s2]))
    V_new = Q.max(1)
    if np.abs(V_new - V).max() < 1e-8:
        break
    V = V_new
print(sweep, V.reshape(4, 4).round(2))
print(np.array(list("↑→↓←"))[Q.argmax(1)].reshape(4, 4))
Ready to use Python · Gymnasium (FrozenLake model)
import gymnasium as gym
import numpy as np

env = gym.make("FrozenLake-v1", is_slippery=True)
P = env.unwrapped.P                                    # P[s][a] = [(prob, next_state, reward, done), ...]
nS, nA, gamma = env.observation_space.n, env.action_space.n, 0.99
V = np.zeros(nS)
for _ in range(1000):
    Q = np.array([[sum(p * (r + gamma * V[s2] * (not d)) for p, s2, r, d in P[s][a]) for a in range(nA)] for s in range(nS)])
    if np.abs(Q.max(1) - V).max() < 1e-10:
        break
    V = Q.max(1)
policy = Q.argmax(1)
wins = 0
for ep in range(1000):
    s, _ = env.reset(seed=ep)
    done = False
    while not done:
        s, r, term, trunc, _ = env.step(int(policy[s]))
        done = term or trunc
    wins += r
print(V.reshape(4, 4).round(3), "success rate", wins / 1000)

Q-learning Advanced

Value iteration needs the model $P$. Q-learning learns from experience alone. The agent acts, observes a transition $(s, a, r, s')$, and nudges its estimate toward the Bellman target:

$$Q(s, a) \leftarrow Q(s, a) + \alpha\Big[r + \gamma\max_{a'}Q(s', a') - Q(s, a)\Big]$$

The bracket is the temporal-difference (TD) error. Q-learning is off-policy: its target uses the best next action, whatever the agent does next, so it learns the optimal policy while exploring with ε-greedy. SARSA uses the action the agent takes, $r + \gamma Q(s', a')$, and learns the value of the exploring policy instead, which keeps it farther from cliffs.

Deep Q-networks (DQN) replace the table with a neural network $Q_\theta(s, a)$ and minimize $\big(r + \gamma\max_{a'}Q_{\theta^-}(s', a') - Q_\theta(s, a)\big)^2$. Two tricks stabilize training: a replay buffer samples past transitions at random to break correlations, and a target network $\theta^-$ copies $\theta$ only every few thousand steps.

Analogy. Learn a city by driving a taxi without a map. Each ride teaches you a little about which turn at which corner gets you to the destination fastest, and you update your mental map after each turn.

The agent (black dot) starts at the bottom left and learns by trial and error, with no access to the transition model. Cell color shows $\max_a Q(s, a)$ and arrows the greedy action. Watch the values spread backward from the goal, one episode at a time.

From scratch Python · NumPy + Gymnasium env
import gymnasium as gym
import numpy as np

env = gym.make("FrozenLake-v1", is_slippery=False)
Q = np.zeros((env.observation_space.n, env.action_space.n))
alpha, gamma, eps = 0.5, 0.95, 0.1
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
for ep in range(2000):
    s, _ = env.reset(seed=ep)
    done = False
    while not done:
        best = np.flatnonzero(Q[s] == Q[s].max())
        a = rng.integers(4) if rng.random() < eps else rng.choice(best)   # ε-greedy, random tie-break
        s2, r, term, trunc, _ = env.step(int(a))
        target = r + gamma * Q[s2].max() * (not term)                    # Bellman target
        Q[s, a] += alpha * (target - Q[s, a])                            # TD update
        s, done = s2, term or trunc
print(np.array(list("←↓→↑"))[Q.argmax(1)].reshape(4, 4))
print(Q.max(1).reshape(4, 4).round(2))
Ready to use Python · Stable-Baselines3 (DQN)
from stable_baselines3 import DQN
from stable_baselines3.common.evaluation import evaluate_policy

model = DQN("MlpPolicy", "CartPole-v1", learning_rate=1e-3, buffer_size=50_000, learning_starts=1000,
            target_update_interval=500, exploration_fraction=0.2, seed=0, verbose=0)
model.learn(total_timesteps=8_000)                     # use about 100_000 for a strong agent
mean, std = evaluate_policy(model, model.get_env(), n_eval_episodes=10)
print(f"episode return {mean:.1f} ± {std:.1f}")
# model.save("dqn_cartpole"); DQN.load("dqn_cartpole")

Policy gradients Advanced

Policy-gradient methods skip the value table and parametrize the policy $\pi_\theta(a\mid s)$ itself, often as a softmax over action scores. They climb the gradient of the expected return $J(\theta) = \mathbb E_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}[R(\tau)]$. The log-derivative trick, $\nabla_\theta\pi_\theta = \pi_\theta\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta$, turns that gradient into an expectation you can sample:

$$\nabla_\theta J(\theta) = \mathbb E_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}\Big[\sum_t\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a_t\mid s_t)\,G_t\Big]$$

REINFORCE estimates it from sampled episodes: raise the log-probability of each action in proportion to the return that followed it. The estimate is unbiased but noisy. Subtracting a baseline $b(s_t)$ from $G_t$ keeps it unbiased and cuts the variance. The natural baseline is the value $V(s_t)$, which gives the advantage $A_t = G_t - V(s_t)$: how much better the action did than expected. Actor-critic methods learn $V$ with a second network (the critic) and use it as the baseline.

Analogy. Train a dog with treats: when an action earns a treat, make that action more likely next time. The baseline asks whether this went better than usual, so an ordinary treat on an ordinary day changes little.

REINFORCE on a 3-armed bandit, 10 runs per method; each line tracks the probability of picking the best arm. Add a constant to all rewards: without a baseline the runs scatter, with a baseline they stay tight.

From scratch Python · NumPy + Gymnasium env
import gymnasium as gym
import numpy as np

env = gym.make("CartPole-v1")
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
theta, lr, gamma = np.zeros(4), 0.01, 0.99
sig = lambda z: 1 / (1 + np.exp(-z))
history = []
for ep in range(500):
    s, _ = env.reset(seed=ep)
    states, actions, rewards = [], [], []
    done = False
    while not done:
        p = sig(theta @ s)                              # π(a = 1 | s)
        a = int(rng.random() < p)
        states.append(s); actions.append(a)
        s, r, term, trunc, _ = env.step(a)
        rewards.append(r); done = term or trunc
    G = np.zeros(len(rewards)); run = 0.0
    for t in reversed(range(len(rewards))):             # returns-to-go
        run = rewards[t] + gamma * run; G[t] = run
    A = (G - G.mean()) / (G.std() + 1e-8)               # baseline: subtract the mean return
    for s_t, a_t, A_t in zip(states, actions, A):
        theta += lr * A_t * (a_t - sig(theta @ s_t)) * s_t   # ∇ log π(a|s) · advantage
    history.append(len(rewards))
print("mean episode length, first 50:", np.mean(history[:50]), "last 50:", np.mean(history[-50:]))
Ready to use Python · Stable-Baselines3 (A2C)
from stable_baselines3 import A2C
from stable_baselines3.common.evaluation import evaluate_policy

model = A2C("MlpPolicy", "CartPole-v1", seed=0, verbose=0)    # actor-critic: policy gradient with a learned baseline
model.learn(total_timesteps=20_000)
mean, std = evaluate_policy(model, model.get_env(), n_eval_episodes=10)
print(f"episode return {mean:.1f} ± {std:.1f}")   # 500 is the maximum

Proximal policy optimization Expert

A policy-gradient step can be too large: one bad update collapses the policy, and the data it collects next is bad too. PPO limits how far each update moves the policy. With the probability ratio $r_t(\theta) = \pi_\theta(a_t\mid s_t)/\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}(a_t\mid s_t)$ and advantage estimates $\hat A_t$, it maximizes the clipped objective:

$$L^{\text{CLIP}}(\theta) = \mathbb E_t\Big[\min\big(r_t(\theta)\hat A_t,\ \operatorname{clip}\big(r_t(\theta), 1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon\big)\hat A_t\big)\Big]$$

For a good action ($\hat A_t > 0$), the objective stops rewarding increases of $r_t$ past $1 + \epsilon$. For a bad action, it stops rewarding decreases below $1 - \epsilon$. The min makes the objective a pessimistic bound, so the update gains nothing by moving far from the old policy ($\epsilon \approx 0.2$). PPO collects a batch with the old policy, then runs several epochs of minibatch gradient ascent on $L^{\text{CLIP}}$, plus a value loss and an entropy bonus.

Most implementations compute advantages with generalized advantage estimation (GAE), an exponentially weighted sum of TD errors $\delta_t = r_t + \gamma V(s_{t+1}) - V(s_t)$: $\hat A_t = \sum_{l\ge0}(\gamma\lambda)^l\delta_{t+l}$, where $\lambda$ trades bias against variance. TRPO, PPO's predecessor, enforced a hard limit on the KL divergence between old and new policies instead of clipping.

Analogy. A driving instructor with a second brake pedal: the student may improve, but the instructor stops any single lesson from changing the driving too much at once.

The clipped objective for one action as a function of the ratio $r$. Dashed: the unclipped $r\hat A$. Solid: PPO's objective. On the flat part the gradient is zero, so the update stops pushing. Flip the sign of $\hat A$ to see the other side.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def clipped_objective(ratio, adv, eps=0.2):
    return np.minimum(ratio * adv, np.clip(ratio, 1 - eps, 1 + eps) * adv)

def gae(rewards, values, gamma=0.99, lam=0.95):
    """values has one more entry than rewards (the value of the final state)."""
    adv, run = np.zeros(len(rewards)), 0.0
    for t in reversed(range(len(rewards))):
        delta = rewards[t] + gamma * values[t + 1] - values[t]      # TD error
        run = delta + gamma * lam * run
        adv[t] = run
    return adv

r = np.array([0.6, 0.9, 1.0, 1.3, 1.6])
print(clipped_objective(r, adv=1.0), clipped_objective(r, adv=-1.0))
print(gae(np.ones(5), np.array([4.0, 3.5, 3.0, 2.0, 1.0, 0.0])).round(3))
Ready to use Python · Stable-Baselines3 (PPO)
from stable_baselines3 import PPO
from stable_baselines3.common.evaluation import evaluate_policy

model = PPO("MlpPolicy", "CartPole-v1", clip_range=0.2, gae_lambda=0.95, n_steps=512, batch_size=64,
            n_epochs=10, learning_rate=3e-4, seed=0, verbose=0)
model.learn(total_timesteps=20_000)
mean, std = evaluate_policy(model, model.get_env(), n_eval_episodes=10)
print(f"episode return {mean:.1f} ± {std:.1f}")

RLHF and direct preference optimization Expert

Language models learn to follow instructions in stages: pretraining (), supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on demonstrations, then preference optimization. People compare two responses to the same prompt $x$ and mark the preferred one $y_w$ over $y_l$. A reward model $r_\phi$ learns those preferences with the Bradley–Terry model:

$$P(y_w \succ y_l\mid x) = \sigma\big(r_\phi(x, y_w) - r_\phi(x, y_l)\big), \qquad L_{\text{RM}} = -\log\sigma\big(r_\phi(x, y_w) - r_\phi(x, y_l)\big)$$

RLHF then maximizes the reward with PPO (). A KL penalty keeps the model close to the reference model $\pi_{\text{ref}}$ (the SFT model), so it can't exploit flaws in the reward model:

$$\max_\pi\ \mathbb E_{x,\,y\sim\pi}\big[r_\phi(x, y)\big] - \beta\,D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\pi(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,\pi_{\text{ref}}(\cdot\mid x)\big)$$

This objective has a closed-form optimum, $\pi^*(y\mid x) \propto \pi_{\text{ref}}(y\mid x)\exp\big(r(x, y)/\beta\big)$. Solve it for $r$, substitute into the Bradley–Terry loss, and the reward model drops out. Direct preference optimization (DPO) trains the policy on the preference pairs with a classification loss:

$$L_{\text{DPO}} = -\log\sigma\Big(\beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y_w\mid x)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_w\mid x)} - \beta\log\frac{\pi_\theta(y_l\mid x)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_l\mid x)}\Big)$$

DPO raises the likelihood of preferred responses relative to rejected ones, measured against the reference model, with no sampling and no separate reward model.

Analogy. Tasting two dishes and saying which you prefer is easier than scoring each from 1 to 10. DPO trains straight from those "A beats B" choices while staying close to the original cook (the reference model).

Four candidate responses with reference probabilities (gray) and three preference pairs: A ≻ B, A ≻ C, C ≻ D. Train with the DPO loss and watch the policy (blue) shift probability toward preferred responses. The readout shows each pair's Bradley–Terry probability under the implicit reward $\beta\log(\pi_\theta/\pi_{\text{ref}})$.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def dpo_loss(logp_w, logp_l, ref_w, ref_l, beta=0.1):
    """Inputs are summed log-probabilities of the chosen (w) and rejected (l) responses."""
    margin = beta * ((logp_w - ref_w) - (logp_l - ref_l))     # difference of implicit rewards
    return np.logaddexp(0, -margin), 1 / (1 + np.exp(-margin))   # −log σ(margin), P(w ≻ l)

ref_w, ref_l = -42.0, -40.0                            # the reference model slightly prefers the rejected answer
for shift in (0.0, 5.0, 20.0):                         # the policy raises log π(w) by `shift`
    loss, p = dpo_loss(ref_w + shift, ref_l, ref_w, ref_l)
    print(shift, round(float(loss), 4), round(float(p), 4))
Ready to use Python · TRL
from datasets import Dataset
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer
from trl import DPOConfig, DPOTrainer

name = "gpt2"                                          # any causal LM, e.g. "Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B-Instruct"
model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(name)
tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(name)
tok.pad_token = tok.pad_token or tok.eos_token
data = Dataset.from_list([
    {"prompt": "What is 2 + 2?", "chosen": " 4.", "rejected": " 5."},
    {"prompt": "Capital of Cambodia?", "chosen": " Phnom Penh.", "rejected": " Bangkok."},
])
args = DPOConfig(output_dir="dpo-out", beta=0.1, per_device_train_batch_size=2, max_steps=2, logging_steps=1,
                 bf16=False, report_to="none")   # bf16=True on a recent GPU
trainer = DPOTrainer(model=model, args=args, train_dataset=data, processing_class=tok)   # ref model: a frozen copy by default
trainer.train()
print(trainer.state.log_history[-1])

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

Why does ε-greedy's regret grow in proportion to $T$ while UCB's grows like $\log T$?

ε-greedy keeps picking random arms with probability ε forever, so it loses a fixed amount per round. UCB's exploration bonus shrinks as data comes in, so it stops wasting pulls on arms it knows are worse.

What makes Q-learning off-policy?

Its target uses the best next action, $r + \gamma\max_{a'}Q(s', a')$, whatever the agent does next, so it learns the optimal policy while it explores with ε-greedy.

Why does PPO clip the probability ratio?

One batch can push the policy far from the policy that collected the data, and performance can collapse. Clipping $r_t = \pi_\theta(a_t\mid s_t)/\pi_{\text{old}}(a_t\mid s_t)$ to $[1 - \epsilon, 1 + \epsilon]$ removes the incentive to move beyond that range.

What does DPO drop from RLHF?

The separate reward model and the reinforcement learning loop. It trains the policy on preference pairs with a classification-style loss derived from the same KL-regularized objective.

  • huggingface/trl: the Hugging Face library for training language models with rewards and preferences; its DPO trainer lives in trl/trainer/dpo_trainer.py. Project: fine-tune a small open model (0.5 to 1 billion parameters) with DPO on a public preference dataset, then compare its answers with the original model. Practices .

Part VIII. Graphs

This part starts with the matrix view of a graph and random walks on it, the tools behind PageRank, then builds graph neural networks on the same matrices.

Graphs and random walks

Graphs and adjacency matrices Beginner

A graph is a set of nodes joined by edges: people and friendships, web pages and links, atoms and bonds, road junctions and roads. Edges can have a direction (a link from page $i$ to page $j$) and a weight (the length of a road). You store a graph with $n$ nodes as an $n\times n$ adjacency matrix:

$$A_{ij} = \begin{cases}1 & \text{if an edge joins } i \text{ to } j\\ 0 & \text{otherwise}\end{cases}, \qquad d_i = \sum_j A_{ij}, \qquad (A^k)_{ij} = \#\{\text{walks of length } k \text{ from } i \text{ to } j\}$$

For an undirected graph, $A$ is symmetric. The row sums are the degrees $d_i$, the number of neighbors of each node. Matrix powers count walks: $(A^2)_{ij} = \sum_m A_{im}A_{mj}$ adds one for each middle node $m$ that links $i$ to $j$, and the same argument repeats for each extra step. Real graphs are sparse, with a few neighbors per node, so large ones live in edge lists: a dense matrix for a million nodes would hold $10^{12}$ entries.

Breadth-first search (BFS) finds shortest paths in an unweighted graph. Visit the start node, then its neighbors, then their unvisited neighbors, and record each node's distance on its first visit; the run takes time $O(n + |E|)$ for $|E|$ edges. With weighted edges, Dijkstra's algorithm replaces the queue with a priority queue. The Laplacian $L = D - A$, with $D$ the diagonal matrix of degrees, links graphs back to eigenvectors (): the eigenvector of its second-smallest eigenvalue splits a graph into two halves with few edges between them, a method called spectral clustering.

Analogy. A class's friendship table: row $i$, column $j$ holds a 1 if students $i$ and $j$ are friends, and a student's row sum counts their friends. Multiply the table by itself, and each entry counts the friends two students share, which is the number of ways a note can pass from one to the other through a single friend.

Click a node to start from it. The matrix shows $A^k$: entry $(i, j)$ counts the walks of length $k$ from $i$ to $j$, and the orange row belongs to the start node. Blue nodes are reachable by a walk of $k$ steps; green labels give the BFS distance from the start.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np
from collections import deque

edges = [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 2), (1, 3), (3, 4), (4, 5), (4, 6), (5, 6), (6, 7)]
n = 8
A = np.zeros((n, n), dtype=int)
for i, j in edges:
    A[i, j] = A[j, i] = 1                          # undirected: the matrix is symmetric

print("degrees:", A.sum(1))
A3 = np.linalg.matrix_power(A, 3)
print("walks of length 3 from node 0 to node 3:", A3[0, 3])   # (A^k)_ij counts walks of length k

def bfs(A, source):
    """Shortest path lengths (number of edges) from source, by breadth-first search."""
    dist = {source: 0}
    queue = deque([source])
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in np.flatnonzero(A[u]):             # neighbors of u
            if v not in dist:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    return [dist.get(v, -1) for v in range(len(A))]

print("distances from node 0:", bfs(A, 0))
L = np.diag(A.sum(1)) - A                          # graph Laplacian D - A
vals, vecs = np.linalg.eigh(L)
print("Fiedler vector signs split the graph:", (vecs[:, 1] > 0).astype(int))   # spectral clustering in one line
Ready to use Python · NetworkX
import networkx as nx

G = nx.Graph([(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 2), (1, 3), (3, 4), (4, 5), (4, 6), (5, 6), (6, 7)])
A = nx.to_numpy_array(G, nodelist=range(8), dtype=int)
print(A)
print(dict(G.degree()))
print(nx.single_source_shortest_path_length(G, 0))
print(nx.shortest_path(G, 0, 7), nx.diameter(G))
print([sorted(c) for c in nx.community.louvain_communities(G, seed=0)])   # community detection
# Large graphs: keep the sparse form, nx.to_scipy_sparse_array(G); a dense n × n matrix needs n² numbers.

Random walks and PageRank Intermediate

Picture a surfer who clicks a random link on each page. Pages that many links point to collect the most visits, and a link from a page the surfer visits often counts for more. PageRank (Brin and Page, 1998) ranks pages by the share of time this surfer spends on each. The walk is a Markov chain () with transition matrix $P_{ij} = A_{ij}/d_i$, where $d_i$ counts the links leaving page $i$.

Two things break the plain walk. A page with no outgoing links traps the surfer, and a group of pages that link to each other and nowhere else absorbs all the time. PageRank fixes both with teleporting: with probability $\alpha$ (0.85 in the original) the surfer follows a link, and otherwise jumps to a page chosen at random. From a dead end the surfer jumps. The ranking $\boldsymbol\pi$ is the stationary distribution of this walk:

$$\boldsymbol\pi = \alpha\,\boldsymbol\pi P + \frac{1-\alpha}{n}\,\mathbf 1^\top, \qquad \boldsymbol\pi^{(t+1)} = \alpha\,\boldsymbol\pi^{(t)} P + \frac{1-\alpha}{n}\,\mathbf 1^\top$$

The right-hand form is power iteration: start from the uniform vector and repeat. Each step shrinks the error by at least a factor $\alpha$, so 50 steps shrink it by a factor of about 3,000 at $\alpha = 0.85$. In matrix terms, $\boldsymbol\pi$ is the leading left eigenvector () of the Google matrix $G = \alpha P + \frac{1-\alpha}{n}\mathbf 1\mathbf 1^\top$, with eigenvalue 1. Teleporting lets the surfer reach each page from each other page, which guarantees that this eigenvector is unique.

Personalized PageRank teleports back to one chosen node instead of a random one, so it scores nodes by closeness to that node; recommenders use it to find items like the one you are viewing. Random walks also turn graphs into sequences: DeepWalk and node2vec run short walks from each node and train word2vec () on them, with nodes as the words, which gives each node an embedding.

Analogy. A tourist wanders a city: at each corner they walk down a random street, and now and then they take a taxi to a random spot. Over a long trip, the share of time they spend at each square is its PageRank. Squares that many streets lead to rank highest, and streets from busy squares count for more. The taxi rides keep the tourist from getting stuck in a dead-end alley.

Each node is a page, arrows are links, and node size shows PageRank. Press Reset and then Step to watch power iteration converge from the uniform start, or let the random surfer walk and compare its visit shares (orange) with PageRank (blue). Page 7 has no links out, so the surfer jumps from it to any page. Lower $\alpha$ and the ranks even out.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

links = {0: [1, 2], 1: [2], 2: [0], 3: [2], 4: [2, 3], 5: [4], 6: [4, 5], 7: []}   # page -> pages it links to
n, alpha = 8, 0.85
P = np.zeros((n, n))
for i, outs in links.items():
    P[i, outs if outs else range(n)] = 1 / (len(outs) or n)   # a dead end (no links) jumps anywhere

pi = np.full(n, 1 / n)
for t in range(100):                               # power iteration: follow a link, or teleport
    new = alpha * pi @ P + (1 - alpha) / n
    if np.abs(new - pi).sum() < 1e-12:
        break
    pi = new
print(f"{t} iterations:", pi.round(3), pi.sum())

G = alpha * P + (1 - alpha) / n                    # the Google matrix: rows sum to 1
vals, vecs = np.linalg.eig(G.T)
v = np.real(vecs[:, np.argmax(np.real(vals))])
print("leading eigenvector:", (v / v.sum()).round(3))   # the same ranking

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)                     # or simulate the random surfer and count visits
page, visits = 0, np.zeros(n)
for _ in range(200_000):
    page = rng.choice(n, p=P[page]) if rng.random() < alpha else rng.integers(n)
    visits[page] += 1
print("surfer's visit shares:", (visits / visits.sum()).round(3))
Ready to use Python · NetworkX
import networkx as nx

links = {0: [1, 2], 1: [2], 2: [0], 3: [2], 4: [2, 3], 5: [4], 6: [4, 5], 7: []}
G = nx.DiGraph()
G.add_nodes_from(links)
G.add_edges_from((i, j) for i, outs in links.items() for j in outs)
pr = nx.pagerank(G, alpha=0.85)                    # dead ends jump uniformly, as above
print({k: round(v, 3) for k, v in pr.items()})
ppr = nx.pagerank(G, alpha=0.85, personalization={6: 1})   # personalized: always teleport back to page 6
print({k: round(v, 3) for k, v in ppr.items()})     # "pages related to 6", as used in recommendation

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

In an undirected graph, what does the diagonal entry $(A^2)_{ii}$ count?

The walks of length 2 from $i$ back to $i$, one per neighbor, so $(A^2)_{ii} = d_i$, the degree.

Why does PageRank need teleporting?

Without it, dead-end pages trap the surfer and closed groups of pages absorb all the time, so the walk may have no unique stationary distribution. Teleporting makes each page reachable from each other page.

At $\alpha = 0.85$, about how many power iterations shrink the error by a factor of 1,000?

$0.85^t = 10^{-3}$ gives $t = \ln 1000/\ln(1/0.85) \approx 43$.

Graph neural networks

Message passing and graph convolutions Expert

Many datasets are graphs: molecules (atoms and bonds), social networks, citation networks, road maps. A graph neural network computes a vector $\mathbf h_v$ for each node $v$ by message passing. In each layer, every node gathers messages from its neighbors $\mathcal N(v)$, aggregates them with an order-independent function (sum, mean or max), and updates its own vector:

$$\mathbf h_v^{(l+1)} = \phi\Big(W_{\text{self}}\,\mathbf h_v^{(l)} + W_{\text{nbr}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal N(v)}\mathbf h_u^{(l)}\Big)$$

After $L$ layers, each node's vector depends on its $L$-hop neighborhood. The graph convolutional network (GCN) of Kipf and Welling writes one layer for all nodes in matrix form:

$$H^{(l+1)} = \phi\big(\tilde D^{-1/2}\tilde A\,\tilde D^{-1/2}H^{(l)}W^{(l)}\big), \qquad \tilde A = A + I$$

$A$ is the adjacency matrix (), the added $I$ gives each node a self-loop, and $\tilde D$ is the diagonal degree matrix of $\tilde A$. The symmetric normalization averages over neighbors, so high-degree nodes don't blow up. Graph attention networks (GAT) learn the neighbor weights with attention (), and a transformer is a GNN on a fully connected graph.

Too many layers cause over-smoothing: repeated averaging drives all node vectors toward the same value, and nodes become indistinguishable. For a graph-level prediction, such as whether a molecule is toxic, pool the node vectors into one vector with a sum or mean, then classify.

Analogy. Gossip in a village: each round, every person updates their opinion from their friends' opinions. After a few rounds, news has traveled a few friendships away; after too many, everyone holds the same opinion (over-smoothing).

Two communities joined by one edge. Each node holds one number (color and label). Each press applies one normalized-averaging layer, $\tilde D^{-1/2}\tilde A\tilde D^{-1/2}\mathbf h$. Information spreads one hop per layer; after many layers both communities blur into a single profile set only by node degree (over-smoothing).

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

edges = [(0, 1), (0, 2), (1, 2), (1, 3), (2, 4), (3, 4), (3, 5), (4, 5), (0, 5), (0, 6),
         (6, 7), (6, 8), (7, 8), (7, 9), (8, 10), (9, 10), (9, 11), (10, 11), (6, 11)]
n = 12
A = np.zeros((n, n))
for a, b in edges:
    A[a, b] = A[b, a] = 1
A_tilde = A + np.eye(n)                                   # self-loops
d = A_tilde.sum(1)
A_hat = A_tilde / np.sqrt(np.outer(d, d))                 # D^-1/2 Ã D^-1/2

def gcn_layer(H, W):
    return np.maximum(0, A_hat @ H @ W)                   # H' = ReLU(Â H W)

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
H = np.eye(n)                                             # one-hot node features
H = gcn_layer(gcn_layer(H, rng.normal(0, 0.5, (n, 8))), rng.normal(0, 0.5, (8, 2)))
print(H.shape)
h = np.eye(n)[0]                                          # over-smoothing: repeat the averaging
for k in (1, 3, 30):
    print(k, (np.linalg.matrix_power(A_hat, k) @ h / np.sqrt(d)).round(3)[:6])
Ready to use Python · PyTorch Geometric
import torch
import torch.nn.functional as F
from torch_geometric.datasets import KarateClub
from torch_geometric.nn import GCNConv

data = KarateClub()[0]                                    # 34 members, 4 communities, ships with the library
class GCN(torch.nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_in, d_h, n_cls):
        super().__init__()
        self.c1, self.c2 = GCNConv(d_in, d_h), GCNConv(d_h, n_cls)
    def forward(self, x, edge_index):
        return self.c2(F.relu(self.c1(x, edge_index)), edge_index)

torch.manual_seed(0)
model = GCN(data.num_features, 16, int(data.y.max()) + 1)
opt = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=0.01, weight_decay=5e-4)
for epoch in range(200):
    opt.zero_grad()
    out = model(data.x, data.edge_index)
    F.cross_entropy(out[data.train_mask], data.y[data.train_mask]).backward()   # only 4 labeled nodes
    opt.step()
print("accuracy on all nodes:", (out.argmax(1) == data.y).float().mean().item())
# Citation graphs: torch_geometric.datasets.Planetoid(root="data", name="Cora")

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

After three GCN layers, which nodes can influence a node's vector?

The nodes within three hops, since each layer passes messages one hop.

What is over-smoothing?

Repeated neighbor averaging drives all node vectors toward the same value, so a deep GCN makes nodes hard to tell apart.

Part IX. Geospatial analysis

Geospatial data carries a location on the Earth: a GPS track, a land parcel, a satellite image. Location brings three facts that ordinary tables lack. The Earth is curved, flat maps distort it, and nearby places tend to look alike. This part covers the math behind each fact and the methods built on it.

Locations and maps

Coordinates and great-circle distance Beginner

A location on the Earth is a pair of angles. Latitude $\varphi$ runs from −90° at the South Pole to +90° at the North Pole, and longitude $\lambda$ from −180° to +180° east of Greenwich. GPS reports both on the WGS84 reference ellipsoid (EPSG:4326).

Degrees are angles, and their length on the ground varies. One degree of latitude spans about 111.2 km everywhere, but one degree of longitude spans $111.2\cos\varphi$ km: 109 km at Phnom Penh and 0 at the poles. Euclidean distance on raw (latitude, longitude) pairs gets this wrong. The shortest path along the surface follows a great circle, and the haversine formula gives its length on a sphere of radius $R \approx 6371$ km:

$$a = \sin^2\frac{\Delta\varphi}{2} + \cos\varphi_1\cos\varphi_2\sin^2\frac{\Delta\lambda}{2}, \qquad d = 2R\arcsin\sqrt a$$

Phnom Penh to Bangkok comes out at 538 km. The sphere is an approximation: the Earth bulges at the equator, and geodesic libraries that use the ellipsoid differ by up to about 0.5%.

Analogy. Stretch a string between two cities on a globe: it marks the shortest route. Lay a flat map on the table and that string becomes a curve, which is why flights from East Asia to North America pass near Alaska.

Pick two cities or drag the markers. The orange curve is the great-circle route drawn on a flat map; the dashed line is the straight line on that map, a longer route on the real Earth. The readout compares the haversine distance with a naive estimate from degrees.

In practice: use haversine distance, or a projected coordinate system (), when you run k-NN () or clustering () on GPS points. scikit-learn's BallTree accepts metric="haversine" with coordinates in radians.

From scratch Python · plain Python
import math

def haversine_km(lat1, lon1, lat2, lon2, R=6371.0):
    p1, p2 = math.radians(lat1), math.radians(lat2)
    dphi, dlmb = p2 - p1, math.radians(lon2 - lon1)
    a = math.sin(dphi / 2) ** 2 + math.cos(p1) * math.cos(p2) * math.sin(dlmb / 2) ** 2
    return 2 * R * math.asin(math.sqrt(a))

phnom_penh, bangkok, new_york = (11.56, 104.92), (13.76, 100.50), (40.71, -74.01)
print(round(haversine_km(*phnom_penh, *bangkok)), round(haversine_km(*phnom_penh, *new_york)))
print(round(111.2 * math.cos(math.radians(11.56)), 1), "km per degree of longitude at Phnom Penh")
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn + pyproj
import numpy as np
from pyproj import Geod
from sklearn.metrics.pairwise import haversine_distances

cities = np.radians([[11.56, 104.92], [13.76, 100.50], [40.71, -74.01]])   # [lat, lon] in radians
print((haversine_distances(cities) * 6371).round(0))                       # all pairs, sphere
geod = Geod(ellps="WGS84")                                                  # ellipsoid: more accurate
_, _, meters = geod.inv(104.92, 11.56, 100.50, 13.76)                       # lon, lat order
print(round(meters / 1000, 1), "km on the WGS84 ellipsoid")
# k-NN on GPS points: BallTree(np.radians(latlon), metric="haversine") from sklearn.neighbors

Map projections Intermediate

A map projection turns $(\varphi, \lambda)$ into flat coordinates $(x, y)$. Gauss proved that no flat map of a sphere can keep all distances right, so each projection chooses what to preserve. Three cylindrical projections show the trade-offs:

$$\text{equirectangular: } x = R\lambda,\ y = R\varphi \qquad \text{Mercator: } x = R\lambda,\ y = R\ln\tan\Big(\frac\pi4 + \frac\varphi2\Big) \qquad \text{equal-area: } x = R\lambda,\ y = R\sin\varphi$$

Mercator preserves angles and local shapes (it is conformal), which made it the standard for navigation and for web maps (Web Mercator, EPSG:3857). It inflates areas by $1/\cos^2\varphi$: Greenland looks as big as Africa, which is about 14 times larger. The equal-area projection keeps areas right and squashes shapes near the poles. To measure distances and areas in one region, use a local projected system such as UTM. Cambodia lies in UTM zone 48N (EPSG:32648), where coordinates are in meters.

Analogy. Peel an orange and press the peel flat: it tears or stretches. Every flat map makes the same trade, and each projection chooses what to sacrifice.

Each ellipse is a circle of the same size on the globe (Tissot's indicatrix). Switch projections and watch Mercator blow the circles up toward the poles while the equal-area map squashes them. The slider reads off the area scale at one latitude.

In practice: reproject your data to a projected coordinate system before you compute areas, distances or buffers. GeoPandas does it with to_crs.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

R = 6371.0
lat = np.radians(np.array([0.0, 30.0, 60.0, 80.0]))
lon = np.radians(104.92)
mercator_y = R * np.log(np.tan(np.pi / 4 + lat / 2))
equal_area_y = R * np.sin(lat)
print("Mercator y:", mercator_y.round(0), "area scale:", (1 / np.cos(lat) ** 2).round(2))
print("equal-area y:", equal_area_y.round(0), "x:", round(R * lon))
utm_zone = lambda lon_deg: int((lon_deg + 180) // 6) + 1
print("UTM zone for Phnom Penh:", utm_zone(104.92))      # 48 -> EPSG:32648 in the north
Ready to use Python · pyproj
from pyproj import CRS, Transformer

to_utm = Transformer.from_crs("EPSG:4326", "EPSG:32648", always_xy=True)   # WGS84 lon/lat -> UTM 48N meters
x, y = to_utm.transform(104.92, 11.56)
print(round(x), round(y))
to_web = Transformer.from_crs("EPSG:4326", "EPSG:3857", always_xy=True)    # Web Mercator
print([round(v) for v in to_web.transform(104.92, 11.56)])
print(CRS("EPSG:32648").name)
# GeoPandas: gdf.to_crs(epsg=32648) reprojects a whole table

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

Why shouldn't you run k-NN with Euclidean distance on raw (latitude, longitude) pairs?

A degree of longitude shrinks toward the poles, by the cosine of the latitude, and the Earth is curved. Use the haversine distance, or project the points first.

Web Mercator keeps angles. What does it distort?

Areas, by a factor $1/\cos^2\varphi$: Greenland looks as big as Africa, which is about 14 times larger.

Vector and raster data

Vector data: points, lines and polygons Beginner

Vector data stores geometry as coordinates: points (a school, a well), lines (a road, a river) and polygons (a province, a rice field). In a projected system, the area of a polygon with vertices $(x_1, y_1), \dots, (x_n, y_n)$ comes from the shoelace formula:

$$A = \frac12\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(x_i\,y_{i+1} - x_{i+1}\,y_i\big)\Big|, \qquad (x_{n+1}, y_{n+1}) = (x_1, y_1)$$

To test whether a point lies inside a polygon, cast a ray from the point in a fixed direction and count the polygon edges it crosses. An odd count means inside, an even count outside (the ray-casting rule). A spatial join runs this test at scale, for example to assign each health center to its province. It relies on a spatial index, such as an R-tree or a quadtree, so each point only tests the few polygons whose bounding boxes contain it. Grid systems such as geohash and Uber's H3 cut the Earth into nested cells for the same purpose.

Analogy. Walk in a straight line from where you stand out past the edge of town, and count how many times you cross the town boundary. An odd count means you started inside.

Drag the polygon's corners and the test point. The ray runs to the right, and each crossing gets a mark. The point is inside when the count is odd; the shoelace formula gives the area.

From scratch Python · plain Python
def area(poly):
    """Shoelace formula; vertices in order, coordinates in a projected system."""
    s = sum(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1 for (x1, y1), (x2, y2) in zip(poly, poly[1:] + poly[:1]))
    return abs(s) / 2

def contains(poly, pt):
    """Ray casting: count edge crossings of a ray going right from pt."""
    x, y = pt
    inside = False
    for (x1, y1), (x2, y2) in zip(poly, poly[1:] + poly[:1]):
        if (y1 > y) != (y2 > y) and x < x1 + (y - y1) * (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1):
            inside = not inside
    return inside

poly = [(1.5, 1), (5, 0.8), (8.5, 2), (7, 3.2), (8.4, 5), (4.5, 4.2), (2, 5.2), (3, 3)]
print(area(poly), contains(poly, (4.2, 2.6)), contains(poly, (8.0, 3.5)))
Ready to use Python · Shapely + GeoPandas
import geopandas as gpd
from shapely.geometry import Point, Polygon

province = Polygon([(1.5, 1), (5, 0.8), (8.5, 2), (7, 3.2), (8.4, 5), (4.5, 4.2), (2, 5.2), (3, 3)])
print(province.area, province.contains(Point(4.2, 2.6)), province.buffer(0.5).area)
provinces = gpd.GeoDataFrame({"name": ["A"]}, geometry=[province], crs="EPSG:32648")
clinics = gpd.GeoDataFrame({"clinic": ["c1", "c2", "c3"]}, geometry=[Point(4.2, 2.6), Point(8, 3.5), Point(2.5, 4.5)], crs="EPSG:32648")
joined = gpd.sjoin(clinics, provinces, how="left", predicate="within")      # spatial join, uses an R-tree index
print(joined[["clinic", "name"]])

Raster data and spectral indices Beginner

Raster data stores a grid of cells, each holding a value: an elevation, a temperature, or the light a satellite measured. A satellite image has several bands, one per range of wavelengths. Sentinel-2 records 13 bands at 10 to 60 m per pixel and revisits each place every 5 days; Landsat 8 and 9 record at 30 m. A raster is a 3D array of bands × height × width, so convolutional networks () apply to it as they are.

Plants absorb red light for photosynthesis and reflect most near-infrared (NIR) light. The normalized difference vegetation index compares the two:

$$\text{NDVI} = \frac{\text{NIR} - \text{Red}}{\text{NIR} + \text{Red}} \in [-1, 1]$$

Dense, healthy vegetation scores about 0.6 to 0.9, bare soil about 0.1 to 0.2, and water below 0. The ratio cancels part of the effect of lighting, since a shadow dims both bands. Other indices follow the same pattern, such as NDWI $= (\text{Green} - \text{NIR})/(\text{Green} + \text{NIR})$ for open water.

Analogy. A leaf acts like a mirror for infrared light and a sponge for red light. NDVI compares the two reflections, so a field that glows in infrared and looks dark in red is green and growing.

A synthetic scene with a river, rice fields, forest, bare soil and a town. Switch between true color, single bands and NDVI. Move the pointer to read a pixel, and set the threshold to map vegetation.

In practice: NDVI time series track crop growth and drought, and index bands added as extra input channels can help a land-cover classifier.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
red = rng.uniform(0.02, 0.25, (64, 64))                    # surface reflectance, 0..1
nir = rng.uniform(0.03, 0.5, (64, 64))
with np.errstate(divide="ignore", invalid="ignore"):
    ndvi = np.where(nir + red > 0, (nir - red) / (nir + red), np.nan)
veg = ndvi > 0.4
print(np.nanmin(ndvi).round(2), np.nanmax(ndvi).round(2), "vegetation share:", veg.mean().round(3))
Ready to use Python · rasterio
import numpy as np
import rasterio

with rasterio.open("S2_scene.tif") as src:                 # a 12-band Sentinel-2 GeoTIFF
    red = src.read(4).astype("float32")                     # band 4 = red, band 8 = near-infrared
    nir = src.read(8).astype("float32")
    profile = src.profile
ndvi = (nir - red) / np.maximum(nir + red, 1e-6)
profile.update(count=1, dtype="float32")
with rasterio.open("ndvi.tif", "w", **profile) as dst:     # keeps the georeferencing
    dst.write(ndvi, 1)
print(ndvi.shape, float(ndvi.mean()), profile["crs"])

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

How does the ray-casting rule test whether a point lies inside a polygon?

Cast a ray from the point in a fixed direction and count the polygon edges it crosses: an odd count means inside, an even count outside.

What is the NDVI of a pixel with near-infrared reflectance 0.5 and red reflectance 0.1?

$(0.5 - 0.1)/(0.5 + 0.1) \approx 0.67$, typical of dense, healthy vegetation.

Spatial statistics and machine learning

Spatial autocorrelation and Moran's I Intermediate

Tobler's first law of geography says that near things are more related than distant things. House prices, rainfall and disease rates all cluster in space. Moran's I measures how much each place resembles its neighbors. With values $x_i$, their mean $\bar x$, and spatial weights $w_{ij}$ (for example $w_{ij} = 1$ when cells $i$ and $j$ share an edge, else 0):

$$I = \frac{N}{W}\,\frac{\sum_i\sum_j w_{ij}\,(x_i - \bar x)(x_j - \bar x)}{\sum_i (x_i - \bar x)^2}, \qquad W = \sum_{i,j} w_{ij}$$

$I$ near +1 means neighbors share values (clusters). $I$ near its null expectation, $-1/(N-1)$, means no pattern, and a negative $I$ means neighbors differ, like a checkerboard. To test significance, shuffle the values across locations many times and see where the observed $I$ falls among the shuffled ones (a permutation test, ). Local versions (LISA) locate the hot spots and cold spots.

Analogy. Expensive houses sit next to expensive houses, and flooded fields next to flooded fields. Moran's I scores how much each place looks like its neighbors.

A 12 × 12 grid of values. Pick a pattern, or click cells to flip them between high and low. Clusters give a large positive $I$ and a checkerboard a negative one; the p-value comes from 199 random shuffles.

In practice: check your model's residuals with Moran's I. If they cluster, the model misses a spatial effect, and random cross-validation will overrate it ().

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def morans_i(grid):
    x = grid.ravel().astype(float)
    n_r, n_c = grid.shape
    z = x - x.mean()
    num, W = 0.0, 0
    for i in range(n_r):
        for j in range(n_c):
            for di, dj in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):      # rook neighbors
                a, b = i + di, j + dj
                if 0 <= a < n_r and 0 <= b < n_c:
                    num += z[i * n_c + j] * z[a * n_c + b]
                    W += 1
    return len(x) / W * num / (z @ z)

yy, xx = np.mgrid[:12, :12]
clusters = (((xx - 3) ** 2 + (yy - 3) ** 2 < 10) | ((xx - 8) ** 2 + (yy - 8) ** 2 < 12)).astype(int)
checker = (xx + yy) % 2
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
I = morans_i(clusters)
perm = [morans_i(rng.permutation(clusters.ravel()).reshape(12, 12)) for _ in range(199)]
print(round(I, 3), round(morans_i(checker), 3), "p =", (1 + sum(abs(v) >= abs(I) for v in perm)) / 200)
Ready to use Python · PySAL (esda + libpysal)
import numpy as np
from esda.moran import Moran
from libpysal.weights import lat2W

yy, xx = np.mgrid[:12, :12]
y = (((xx - 3) ** 2 + (yy - 3) ** 2 < 10) | ((xx - 8) ** 2 + (yy - 8) ** 2 < 12)).astype(float).ravel()
w = lat2W(12, 12, rook=True)                            # grid adjacency; for polygons use Queen.from_dataframe(gdf)
mi = Moran(y, w, transformation="B", permutations=199)  # B = binary weights, as in the formula
print(round(mi.I, 3), round(mi.EI, 4), mi.p_sim)

Spatial interpolation: IDW and kriging Advanced

You measure rainfall at a few stations and want a full map. Inverse distance weighting (IDW) averages the stations and gives close ones more weight:

$$\hat z(\mathbf x) = \frac{\sum_i w_i\,z_i}{\sum_i w_i}, \qquad w_i = \frac{1}{d(\mathbf x, \mathbf x_i)^p}$$

The power $p$, often 2, sets how fast influence fades. IDW is simple, but it ignores how the data varies and gives no error bars. Kriging fits that variation first, with a variogram that measures how different two values get as their distance $h$ grows:

$$\gamma(h) = \tfrac12\,\mathbb E\big[(Z(\mathbf x) - Z(\mathbf x + \mathbf h))^2\big] = \sigma^2 - k(h) \quad \text{for a stationary field with covariance } k$$

It then predicts with the best linear unbiased weights under that covariance. Simple kriging gives the same answer as Gaussian process regression (): the prediction is $\mathbf k_*^\top K^{-1}\mathbf z$, and the kriging variance gives a map of the uncertainty. Ordinary kriging also estimates an unknown constant mean.

Analogy. To guess the temperature in a village without a thermometer, you ask nearby villages and trust the closest ones most. IDW does that with a fixed rule; kriging first learns from the data how fast trust should fade with distance.

Click to add stations; each one reads a hidden true field. Compare IDW with kriging (a Gaussian process), scored against the truth in the readout. In kriging mode, switch the view to the standard deviation and watch the uncertainty grow away from the stations.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
truth = lambda x, y: np.sin(1.2 * x) * np.cos(0.9 * y) + 0.6 * np.exp(-((x - 6.5) ** 2 + (y - 2) ** 2) / 1.5)
S = rng.uniform([0.5, 0.4], [9.5, 5.6], (12, 2))           # station coordinates
z = truth(S[:, 0], S[:, 1])
G = np.stack(np.meshgrid(np.linspace(0, 10, 40), np.linspace(0, 6, 24)), -1).reshape(-1, 2)

def idw(G, S, z, p=2):
    d = np.linalg.norm(G[:, None] - S[None], axis=-1) + 1e-12
    w = d ** -p
    return (w @ z) / w.sum(1)

def simple_kriging(G, S, z, ell=1.2, nugget=1e-4):
    k = lambda A, B: np.exp(-((A[:, None] - B[None]) ** 2).sum(-1) / (2 * ell ** 2))
    mu = z.mean()
    K = k(S, S) + nugget * np.eye(len(S))
    Ks = k(G, S)
    pred = mu + Ks @ np.linalg.solve(K, z - mu)
    var = 1 - (Ks * np.linalg.solve(K, Ks.T).T).sum(1)     # kriging variance
    return pred, np.sqrt(np.clip(var, 0, None))

t = truth(G[:, 0], G[:, 1])
pred, std = simple_kriging(G, S, z)
print("RMSE  IDW:", np.sqrt(((idw(G, S, z) - t) ** 2).mean()).round(3), " kriging:", np.sqrt(((pred - t) ** 2).mean()).round(3))
print("largest kriging std:", std.max().round(3))
Ready to use Python · PyKrige
import numpy as np
from pykrige.ok import OrdinaryKriging

rng = np.random.default_rng(3)
x, y = rng.uniform(0.5, 9.5, 30), rng.uniform(0.4, 5.6, 30)
z = np.sin(1.2 * x) * np.cos(0.9 * y)
ok = OrdinaryKriging(x, y, z, variogram_model="gaussian")   # fits the variogram, then krigs
gx, gy = np.linspace(0, 10, 40), np.linspace(0, 6, 24)
zhat, var = ok.execute("grid", gx, gy)                       # predictions and kriging variance on the grid
print(zhat.shape, ok.variogram_model_parameters.round(3), float(np.sqrt(var).max()))

Spatial cross-validation Advanced

Random $k$-fold cross-validation () assumes independent examples. Spatial data breaks that assumption: a test point tends to have training points right next to it that share its value. The model then looks better than it will in a new region. The error estimate leaks.

Spatial cross-validation holds out whole areas instead. Block CV cuts the map into blocks and assigns entire blocks to folds; buffered CV also drops the training points within a distance $r$ of each test point. Choose blocks at least as large as the range of the autocorrelation, the distance at which the variogram () levels off. The spatial estimate looks worse, but it matches the error you will see when you deploy the model in places you never sampled.

Analogy. Testing a model on a station right next to a training station is like grading a student on questions copied from the homework. Block cross-validation holds out whole regions, like an exam with new questions.

A smooth random field sampled at 150 points on the left 70% of the map, fit with k-NN (). Colors show the folds of the chosen scheme. Compare the random and block error estimates with the real error on the unseen right 30% of the map. Raise the correlation range and random CV grows more optimistic: at a range of 0.4 it reports under a third of the real error. Block CV stays closer while the blocks are at least as large as the range.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
W = rng.normal(0, 1 / 0.15, (40, 2)); ph = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi, 40)
field = lambda P: np.sqrt(2 / 40) * np.cos(P @ W.T + ph).sum(1)      # smooth random field, range about 0.15
X = rng.uniform([0, 0], [0.7, 1.0], (150, 2)); y = field(X) + 0.1 * rng.standard_normal(150)
X_new = rng.uniform([0.7, 0], [1.0, 1.0], (300, 2)); y_new = field(X_new)

def knn_predict(Xtr, ytr, Xq, k=5):
    d = ((Xq[:, None] - Xtr[None]) ** 2).sum(-1)
    return ytr[np.argsort(d, 1)[:, :k]].mean(1)

def cv_rmse(folds):
    err = [(knn_predict(X[folds != f], y[folds != f], X[folds == f]) - y[folds == f]) ** 2 for f in range(5)]
    return np.sqrt(np.concatenate(err).mean())

random_folds = rng.permutation(np.arange(150) % 5)
blocks = (X[:, 0] // 0.2).astype(int) * 10 + (X[:, 1] // 0.2).astype(int)        # 0.2 × 0.2 blocks
block_ids = rng.permutation(np.unique(blocks))
block_folds = np.array([np.where(block_ids == b)[0][0] % 5 for b in blocks])     # whole blocks go to one fold
true = np.sqrt(((knn_predict(X, y, X_new) - y_new) ** 2).mean())
print(f"random CV {cv_rmse(random_folds):.3f} | block CV {cv_rmse(block_folds):.3f} | new region {true:.3f}")
Ready to use Python · scikit-learn (GroupKFold)
import numpy as np
from sklearn.model_selection import GroupKFold, KFold, cross_val_score
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsRegressor

rng = np.random.default_rng(1)
W = rng.normal(0, 1 / 0.15, (40, 2)); ph = rng.uniform(0, 2 * np.pi, 40)
X = rng.uniform([0, 0], [0.7, 1.0], (150, 2))
y = np.sqrt(2 / 40) * np.cos(X @ W.T + ph).sum(1) + 0.1 * rng.standard_normal(150)
groups = (X[:, 0] // 0.2).astype(int) * 10 + (X[:, 1] // 0.2).astype(int)        # spatial blocks as groups
model = KNeighborsRegressor(n_neighbors=5)
score = lambda cv, **kw: -cross_val_score(model, X, y, cv=cv, scoring="neg_root_mean_squared_error", **kw).mean()
print("random CV", round(score(KFold(5, shuffle=True, random_state=0)), 3))
print("block CV ", round(score(GroupKFold(5), groups=groups), 3))                  # no block is split across folds

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

What does a Moran's I close to +1 tell you?

Strong positive spatial autocorrelation: similar values cluster in space.

Why does random k-fold cross-validation overrate a model on spatial data?

Nearby points are correlated, so each test point has close neighbors in the training folds and the model interpolates. Spatial (block) cross-validation holds out whole areas.

What does kriging give you that inverse distance weighting doesn't?

An uncertainty at each location, the kriging variance, which comes from the fitted variogram. IDW gives a weighted average with no error estimate.
