Part X. Architectures

Parts II to IX teach algorithms: how a model computes its output and how it learns. An architecture fixes the wiring: which layers a network has and how they connect. Each network in this part recombines algorithms you have met. ResNet is convolution and batch normalization wrapped in residual connections; a Llama model is attention and a gated MLP, repeated 32 times.

The map below links the two halves. Each topic after it draws one architecture with its tensor shapes and parameter counts. The first code block builds the network from basic layers; the second loads the library version.

Algorithms on the left, architectures on the right. Click an architecture to light up the algorithms it combines, or click an algorithm to see which architectures use it. Drag the year slider to watch the families appear.

Convolutional architectures

From LeNet to VGG: reading a CNN Beginner

To read an architecture, follow one input through the network and write down the tensor shape after each layer. LeNet-5 (LeCun and others, 1998) read handwritten digits on bank checks:

$$32{\times}32{\times}1 \xrightarrow{\text{conv }5\times5} 28{\times}28{\times}6 \xrightarrow{\text{pool}} 14{\times}14{\times}6 \xrightarrow{\text{conv }5\times5} 10{\times}10{\times}16 \xrightarrow{\text{pool}} 5{\times}5{\times}16 \xrightarrow{\text{flatten}} 400 \to 120 \to 84 \to 10$$

You get each shape from the conv arithmetic (): a 5 × 5 kernel without padding trims 4 pixels, and a 2 × 2 pool halves the size. Next to each shape, write two counts. A conv layer with $C_{\text{out}}$ kernels of size $K\times K\times C_{\text{in}}$ and an $H\times W$ output, and a dense layer from $n$ to $m$ units, cost

$$\text{params} = K^2C_{\text{in}}C_{\text{out}} + C_{\text{out}}, \quad \text{MACs} = HW\,K^2C_{\text{in}}C_{\text{out}} \qquad\qquad \text{params} = nm + m, \quad \text{MACs} = nm$$

A multiply-add (MAC) is one step $a \leftarrow a + w\,x$; hardware specs count it as 2 FLOPs. LeNet-5 holds 61,706 parameters and needs 416,520 multiply-adds per digit.

VGG-16 (Simonyan and Zisserman, 2014) scales the same recipe up to 224 × 224 color images. Thirteen 3 × 3 conv layers, padded to keep the size, form five stages, and a 2 × 2 max pool ends each stage. The channels double after each pool (64, 128, 256, 512, 512) while the height and width halve, so the cost per layer stays level through the first four stages. Three dense layers follow. The counts show where VGG-16 spends its budget. The conv layers do 99% of the computing, because each kernel slides over thousands of positions. The dense layers hold 89% of the 138 million parameters: the first one maps $7\cdot7\cdot512 = 25{,}088$ numbers to 4,096 units, which takes 103 million weights. Later networks replace the flattening with global average pooling, which averages each feature map to one number. On VGG-16 that cuts the parameters to 15 million and leaves the compute at 15.3 billion multiply-adds.

Analogy. A CNN works like a chain of editors. Street reporters (the first layers) each describe a small patch of the scene. Each editor above them merges neighboring reports into fewer, richer ones that cover more ground. The chief editor (the dense layers) reads the final summary and decides.

Each row is one layer with its output shape. The blue bar shows its share of the parameters, the orange bar its share of the multiply-adds. Switch between LeNet-5 and VGG-16, and tick global average pooling to watch the dense layers' weights disappear. Hover a row to see its formula.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np
from numpy.lib.stride_tricks import sliding_window_view

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)

def conv(x, c_out, k):                         # x: (H, W, C_in), no padding, stride 1
    w, b = rng.normal(0, 0.1, (k, k, x.shape[2], c_out)), np.zeros(c_out)
    patches = sliding_window_view(x, (k, k), axis=(0, 1))          # (H-k+1, W-k+1, C_in, k, k)
    return np.tanh(np.einsum("hwcij,ijcd->hwd", patches, w) + b), w.size + b.size

def pool(x):                                   # 2×2 max pool, stride 2
    H, W, C = x.shape
    return x.reshape(H // 2, 2, W // 2, 2, C).max(axis=(1, 3)), 0

def dense(x, m):
    w, b = rng.normal(0, 0.1, (x.size, m)), np.zeros(m)
    return np.tanh(x.ravel() @ w + b), w.size + b.size

layers = [("conv 5×5, 6", lambda x: conv(x, 6, 5)), ("max pool", pool),
          ("conv 5×5, 16", lambda x: conv(x, 16, 5)), ("max pool", pool),
          ("dense 120", lambda x: dense(x, 120)), ("dense 84", lambda x: dense(x, 84)), ("dense 10", lambda x: dense(x, 10))]
x, total = rng.random((32, 32, 1)), 0
for name, layer in layers:
    x, p = layer(x)
    total += p
    print(f"{name:13s} -> {str(x.shape):12s} {p:6,d} params")
print(f"total {total:,} params")

def vgg16_counts(gap=False):
    """Parameters and multiply-adds of VGG-16 at 224×224, read off its configuration."""
    cfg = [64, 64, "M", 128, 128, "M", 256, 256, 256, "M", 512, 512, 512, "M", 512, 512, 512, "M"]
    H, C, params, macs = 224, 3, 0, 0
    for v in cfg:
        if v == "M":
            H //= 2
            continue
        params += 9 * C * v + v
        macs += H * H * 9 * C * v
        C = v
    dense_layers = [(C, 1000)] if gap else [(H * H * C, 4096), (4096, 4096), (4096, 1000)]
    for n, m in dense_layers:
        params += n * m + m
        macs += n * m
    return params, macs

for gap in (False, True):
    p, m = vgg16_counts(gap)
    print(f"VGG-16, global pooling={gap}: {p:,} params, {m:,} multiply-adds")
Ready to use Python · PyTorch + torchvision
import torch
from torch import nn
from torch.utils.flop_counter import FlopCounterMode
from torchvision.models import vgg16

lenet = nn.Sequential(
    nn.Conv2d(1, 6, 5), nn.Tanh(), nn.MaxPool2d(2),
    nn.Conv2d(6, 16, 5), nn.Tanh(), nn.MaxPool2d(2),
    nn.Flatten(), nn.Linear(400, 120), nn.Tanh(), nn.Linear(120, 84), nn.Tanh(), nn.Linear(84, 10))
x = torch.randn(1, 1, 32, 32)
for layer in lenet:
    x = layer(x)
    print(f"{type(layer).__name__:9s} {tuple(x.shape)}")

count = lambda m: sum(p.numel() for p in m.parameters())
with FlopCounterMode(display=False) as fc:
    lenet(torch.randn(1, 1, 32, 32))
print(f"LeNet-5: {count(lenet):,} params, {fc.get_total_flops() // 2:,} multiply-adds")   # the counter reports 2 FLOPs per multiply-add

vgg = vgg16(weights=None)                   # weights="DEFAULT" downloads the ImageNet weights (about 530 MB)
print(f"VGG-16: {count(vgg):,} params, {count(vgg.classifier):,} of them in the three dense layers")

ResNet: stages and bottleneck blocks Advanced

ResNet (He and others, 2015) arranges residual blocks () in a layout that later CNNs reuse. A stem shrinks the image fast: a 7 × 7 convolution with stride 2 and a 3 × 3 max pool with stride 2 take a 224 × 224 image to 56 × 56 with 64 channels. Four stages follow, each a run of identical blocks at one resolution. The first block of stages 2, 3 and 4 uses stride 2, which halves the height and width, and doubles the channels. The head is a global average pool and one dense layer.

ResNet-18 and 34 use a basic block of two 3 × 3 convolutions. ResNet-50 and deeper use a bottleneck block: a 1 × 1 convolution squeezes the channels by 4, a 3 × 3 convolution works on the narrow tensor, and a second 1 × 1 convolution expands the channels again. Batch normalization () and a ReLU follow each convolution, and the block adds its input before the last ReLU: $\mathbf y = \operatorname{ReLU}(\mathbf x + F(\mathbf x))$. At 256 channels the bottleneck saves a lot:

$$\underbrace{256\cdot64}_{1\times1\text{ squeeze}} + \underbrace{9\cdot64\cdot64}_{3\times3} + \underbrace{64\cdot256}_{1\times1\text{ expand}} = 69{,}632 \qquad\text{against}\qquad 2\cdot9\cdot256^2 = 1{,}179{,}648$$

That is 17 times fewer weights for a block of the same width. If a block changes the shape, the shortcut can't be the identity, so a 1 × 1 convolution with the same stride projects $\mathbf x$ to the new shape (a projection shortcut). The number in the name is the count of weighted layers: ResNet-50 has 1 stem convolution, 16 bottleneck blocks of 3 convolutions and 1 dense layer. Bottlenecks make depth cheap. ResNet-152 is more than nine times deeper than VGG-16, yet it needs fewer than half the parameters and three quarters of the multiply-adds. For your own images, start from ImageNet weights, swap the dense layer for one with your number of classes, and fine-tune (transfer learning).

Analogy. A bottleneck block works like a committee. A full 3 × 3 layer would be a debate among all 256 members; the bottleneck sends 64 delegates to debate, and they report back to the full committee. The shortcut carries the original proposal through unchanged, and the delegates propose amendments to it.

Each colored bar is one residual block; orange blocks change the shape and use a projection shortcut. Pick a depth and compare the totals with VGG-16, then hover a block to see its shapes. The counts match torchvision's models.

From scratch Python · PyTorch
import torch
from torch import nn

class Bottleneck(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, c_in, width, stride):
        super().__init__()
        c_out = 4 * width
        self.f = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Conv2d(c_in, width, 1, bias=False), nn.BatchNorm2d(width), nn.ReLU(),                 # squeeze
            nn.Conv2d(width, width, 3, stride, 1, bias=False), nn.BatchNorm2d(width), nn.ReLU(),     # 3×3 on the narrow tensor
            nn.Conv2d(width, c_out, 1, bias=False), nn.BatchNorm2d(c_out))                          # expand
        self.shortcut = nn.Identity() if stride == 1 and c_in == c_out else nn.Sequential(
            nn.Conv2d(c_in, c_out, 1, stride, bias=False), nn.BatchNorm2d(c_out))                   # projection shortcut

    def forward(self, x):
        return torch.relu(self.shortcut(x) + self.f(x))

def resnet(blocks=(3, 4, 6, 3), classes=1000):
    layers = [nn.Conv2d(3, 64, 7, 2, 3, bias=False), nn.BatchNorm2d(64), nn.ReLU(), nn.MaxPool2d(3, 2, 1)]   # stem
    c_in = 64
    for stage, n in enumerate(blocks):
        width = 64 * 2 ** stage
        for i in range(n):
            layers.append(Bottleneck(c_in, width, stride=2 if i == 0 and stage > 0 else 1))
            c_in = 4 * width
    layers += [nn.AdaptiveAvgPool2d(1), nn.Flatten(), nn.Linear(c_in, classes)]                               # head
    return nn.Sequential(*layers)

model = resnet()                                 # (3, 4, 6, 3) = ResNet-50; (3, 4, 23, 3) = ResNet-101
x = torch.randn(1, 3, 224, 224)
for i in (3, 6, 10, 16, 19):                     # shape after the stem and after each stage
    print(tuple(model[: i + 1](x).shape))
print(f"{sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters()):,} parameters")   # 25,557,032, the same as torchvision's resnet50
Ready to use Python · torchvision
import torch
from torch import nn
from torch.utils.flop_counter import FlopCounterMode
from torchvision.models import resnet18, resnet34, resnet50, resnet101, resnet152

x = torch.randn(1, 3, 224, 224)
for build in (resnet18, resnet34, resnet50, resnet101, resnet152):
    model = build(weights=None).eval()           # weights="DEFAULT" downloads ImageNet weights
    with FlopCounterMode(display=False) as fc:
        model(x)
    print(f"{build.__name__:10s} {sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters()) / 1e6:5.1f} M params, {fc.get_total_flops() / 2e9:5.2f} G multiply-adds")

model = resnet50(weights=None)                   # transfer learning: freeze the body, train a new head
model.requires_grad_(False)
model.fc = nn.Linear(model.fc.in_features, 5)    # 5 classes of your own; the new layer is trainable
print(sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters() if p.requires_grad), "trainable parameters")   # 2048·5 + 5

U-Net: encoder, decoder and skip connections Advanced

Segmentation labels each pixel (), so the output needs the input's resolution. A classifier such as ResNet discards resolution on purpose: by its last stage a 224 × 224 image has shrunk to 7 × 7. U-Net (Ronneberger and others, 2015) adds a mirror image of that path. The encoder repeats two 3 × 3 convolutions and a 2 × 2 max pool, halving the size and doubling the channels at each level, from 64 channels to 1,024 at the bottom. The decoder climbs back: at each level a 2 × 2 transposed convolution with stride 2 doubles the size and halves the channels, and two 3 × 3 convolutions follow. A final 1 × 1 convolution turns the 64 channels into one score per class for each pixel. The network has about 31 million parameters.

At the bottom of the U, each cell summarizes a 16 × 16 patch. The features there tell you what the patch contains but not where an edge falls inside it. Skip connections carry the missing detail across. At each level, the decoder concatenates its upsampled tensor with the encoder tensor of the same size before it convolves:

$$\mathbf d_l = \operatorname{conv}\big(\big[\operatorname{up}(\mathbf d_{l+1});\ \mathbf e_l\big]\big)$$

Here $\mathbf e_l$ is the encoder output at level $l$, and $[\,\cdot\,;\,\cdot\,]$ stacks channels. The encoder tensor still holds the fine detail that pooling removed, so the decoder can place boundaries to the pixel. A transposed convolution with kernel 2 and stride 2 maps each input cell to a 2 × 2 block of outputs through learned weights. U-Net trains well on a few hundred labeled images, which made it the default in medical imaging. Diffusion models () use a U-Net as the noise predictor and feed the time step into each level.

Analogy. Finding an address on an online map: you zoom out to see the whole city and pick the district, then zoom back in. On the way back in, the U-Net doesn't have to guess the street layout from the blurry city view. The skip connections hand it the detailed view it saw on the way out, at the same zoom level.

A simplified U-Net on a 64 × 64 image. The encoder averages each 2 × 2 block, the decoder copies each cell into a 2 × 2 block, and each skip connection hands back the detail lost at its level. A trained U-Net learns its filters, but the information flows the same way. The figure thresholds the output into a mask and scores it against the true mask. Turn skips off, or deepen the U, and watch the small objects vanish.

From scratch Python · PyTorch
import torch
from torch import nn

def double_conv(c_in, c_out):
    return nn.Sequential(nn.Conv2d(c_in, c_out, 3, padding=1), nn.ReLU(), nn.Conv2d(c_out, c_out, 3, padding=1), nn.ReLU())

class UNet(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, c_in=1, classes=2, widths=(64, 128, 256, 512, 1024)):
        super().__init__()
        self.down = nn.ModuleList(double_conv(a, b) for a, b in zip((c_in,) + widths[:-1], widths))
        self.up = nn.ModuleList(nn.ConvTranspose2d(b, a, 2, stride=2) for a, b in zip(widths[:-1], widths[1:]))
        self.dec = nn.ModuleList(double_conv(2 * a, a) for a in widths[:-1])
        self.out = nn.Conv2d(widths[0], classes, 1)      # one score map per class

    def forward(self, x):
        skips = []
        for block in self.down[:-1]:                     # encoder: convolve, keep a copy, halve the size
            x = block(x)
            skips.append(x)
            x = nn.functional.max_pool2d(x, 2)
        x = self.down[-1](x)                             # bottom of the U: 1/16 of the input size
        for up, dec in zip(reversed(self.up), reversed(self.dec)):
            x = dec(torch.cat([up(x), skips.pop()], dim=1))   # decoder: double the size, concatenate the skip
        return self.out(x)

model = UNet()
x = torch.randn(1, 1, 256, 256)
print(model(x).shape)                                    # (1, 2, 256, 256): same size as the input
print(f"{sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters()):,} parameters")
Ready to use Python · segmentation-models-pytorch
import torch
import segmentation_models_pytorch as smp

# U-Net with a ResNet-34 encoder; encoder_weights="imagenet" downloads a pretrained encoder
model = smp.Unet(encoder_name="resnet34", encoder_weights=None, in_channels=3, classes=4)
x = torch.randn(2, 3, 256, 256)
logits = model(x)
print(logits.shape)                                      # (2, 4, 256, 256): one score map per class
masks = torch.randint(0, 4, (2, 256, 256))
print(smp.losses.DiceLoss(mode="multiclass")(logits, masks))
print(f"{sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters()):,} parameters")

YOLO: detection in one pass Advanced

A two-stage detector such as Faster R-CNN first proposes regions that might hold an object, then classifies each region. YOLO (Redmon and others, 2016) does both in one forward pass, which made real-time detection practical. YOLOv1 resizes the image to 448 × 448, runs a CNN, and reads the result as an $S\times S$ grid with $S = 7$. The cell that contains an object's center is responsible for that object. Each cell predicts $B$ boxes, each with five numbers $(x, y, w, h, c)$, plus $C$ class probabilities shared by the cell. The whole output is one tensor:

$$S\times S\times(5B + C) = 7\times7\times(5\cdot2 + 20) = 7\times7\times30$$

$x$ and $y$ place the box center inside the cell, $w$ and $h$ give its size as a fraction of the image, and the confidence $c$ estimates $P(\text{object})\cdot\operatorname{IoU}$ () between the box and the true one. During training, the predictor whose box overlaps the object best takes responsibility for it. The loss sums squared errors over the tensor, with weights: box errors count 5 times, confidence errors in empty cells 0.5 times. It compares $\sqrt w$ and $\sqrt h$, so a 10-pixel error costs more on a small box than on a large one. At test time each box scores $c\cdot P(\text{class}\mid\text{object})$ for each class; you drop the low scores and run non-maximum suppression.

Each cell predicts one class, so two objects whose centers share a cell compete, and YOLOv1 misses flocks of small objects. Later versions fix this. YOLOv2 and v3 predict offsets from anchor boxes, preset box shapes per cell, and v3 predicts at three scales (strides 8, 16 and 32), so small objects get a fine grid. YOLOv8 drops the anchors and predicts each box from its grid point. For a 640 × 640 input its head scores $80^2 + 40^2 + 20^2 = 8{,}400$ grid points, each with 4 box numbers and 80 class scores. Across versions the structure stays the same: a backbone CNN extracts features, a neck mixes them across scales, and a head outputs the boxes.

Analogy. A seating chart for a classroom. You divide the room (the image) into desks (cells), and each student reports whatever lands on their desk: where it sits, how big it is, and what it is. The whole class reports at once, from one glance at the room. If two things land on one desk, the student reports one of them.

Dots mark object centers. Each shaded cell is responsible for the object whose center it holds, and red cells hold two centers. Change the grid size $S$ and the boxes per cell $B$, then click a cell to see its slice of the target tensor.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

S, B, C = 7, 2, 20                                     # grid size, boxes per cell, classes (PASCAL VOC)

def encode(boxes, labels):
    """boxes: (cx, cy, w, h) in [0, 1] image units -> YOLOv1 target tensor S × S × (5B + C)."""
    T = np.zeros((S, S, 5 * B + C))
    for (cx, cy, w, h), k in zip(boxes, labels):
        i, j = int(cy * S), int(cx * S)                # the cell that holds the center is responsible
        if T[i, j, 4] == 1:
            print(f"cell ({i}, {j}) is taken: YOLOv1 loses the object of class {k}")
            continue
        T[i, j, :5] = cx * S - j, cy * S - i, w, h, 1.0   # x, y relative to the cell; w, h relative to the image
        T[i, j, 5 * B + k] = 1.0                          # one class per cell
    return T

def decode(T, threshold=0.5):
    found = []
    for i, j in np.argwhere(T[..., 4] > threshold).tolist():
        x, y, w, h, c = T[i, j, :5].tolist()
        found.append((round((j + x) / S, 3), round((i + y) / S, 3), w, h, int(T[i, j, 5 * B:].argmax())))
    return found

boxes = [(0.30, 0.60, 0.35, 0.40), (0.72, 0.45, 0.20, 0.50), (0.74, 0.47, 0.05, 0.08)]
T = encode(boxes, labels=[6, 14, 2])                   # car, person, and a bird whose center shares the person's cell
print(T.shape, T.size, "numbers")
print(decode(T))
Ready to use Python · ultralytics
import torch
from ultralytics import YOLO

model = YOLO("yolov8n.yaml")                    # the architecture with random weights; "yolov8n.pt" downloads trained weights
model.info()                                     # layers, parameters, GFLOPs
net = model.model.eval()
with torch.no_grad():
    pred = net(torch.zeros(1, 3, 640, 640))[0]   # raw head output, before non-maximum suppression
print(pred.shape)                                # (1, 84, 8400): 4 box numbers + 80 class scores at 80² + 40² + 20² grid points
# Trained detector: results = YOLO("yolov8n.pt")("photo.jpg"); results[0].boxes.xyxy, .cls, .conf

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

Where do VGG-16's parameters live, and where does its compute go?

The three dense layers hold 89% of the 138 million parameters, while the conv layers do 99% of the multiply-adds.

Why does ResNet-50's bottleneck block start and end with 1 × 1 convolutions?

They shrink the channels before the costly 3 × 3 layer and expand them after: 69,632 weights at 256 channels, against 1,179,648 for two 3 × 3 layers.

What would a U-Net lose without its skip connections?

The fine detail that pooling removed. The decoder would produce blocky, blurred masks, and thin or small objects would vanish.

How many numbers does YOLOv1 output for $S = 7$, $B = 2$ and $C = 20$?

$7\times 7\times(5\cdot 2 + 20) = 1{,}470$.

Sequence and attention architectures

WaveNet: dilated causal convolutions Intermediate

Audio has 16,000 or more samples per second, and a model that predicts the next sample needs to see a long stretch of the past. An RNN () steps through the samples one at a time, which makes training slow. WaveNet (van den Oord and others, 2016) uses a stack of one-dimensional convolutions with two changes. A causal convolution looks at the current and earlier steps: you pad $(K-1)D$ zeros on the left and none on the right, so the output at time $t$ can't see the future. A dilated convolution with dilation $D$ spaces its taps $D$ steps apart ():

$$y_t = \sum_{k=0}^{K-1} w_k\,x_{t - kD}$$

WaveNet uses kernel size $K = 2$ and doubles the dilation at each layer: 1, 2, 4, …, 512. The receptive field of a stack of $L$ layers is

$$R = 1 + (K-1)\sum_{l=1}^{L} D_l = 1 + (1 + 2 + 4 + \dots + 512) = 1{,}024$$

Ten layers see 1,024 samples, where ten undilated layers would see 11. The receptive field doubles with each layer, while the parameters grow by one layer's worth. Each layer uses the gated activation of the LSTM (), $\mathbf z = \tanh(W_f * \mathbf x)\odot\sigma(W_g * \mathbf x)$, inside a residual connection (). The output is a softmax over 256 amplitude levels. You train on all time steps in parallel, because the training data holds the true past; to generate, you run one sample at a time and feed each output back in. Temporal convolutional networks (TCNs) reuse this design for sensor data and forecasting.

Analogy. A knockout tournament. Each match combines two players, and each round pairs winners from brackets twice as far apart as the round before. After 10 rounds the champion's result depends on all 1,024 players, though each match looked at two.

Each row is a layer and each column a time step. The orange cone shows the inputs that the selected output can see; no edge points to the future. Add layers, switch the dilation between doubling and none, or click a top node to pick another output.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

def causal_conv(x, w, dilation):
    """y_t = Σ_k w[k] x_{t - k·dilation}, with zeros before t = 0."""
    K, T = len(w), len(x)
    xp = np.concatenate([np.zeros((K - 1) * dilation), x])          # pad on the left only: no future leaks in
    return sum(w[k] * xp[(K - 1 - k) * dilation:(K - 1 - k) * dilation + T] for k in range(K))

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x = np.zeros(4096)
x[0] = 1.0                                                           # an impulse at t = 0
for name, dilations in [("dilated 1, 2, 4, ..., 512", [2 ** l for l in range(10)]), ("plain, dilation 1", [1] * 10)]:
    y = x
    for d in dilations:
        y = causal_conv(y, rng.uniform(0.5, 1.0, size=2), d)        # kernel size 2, positive weights
    print(f"{name}: receptive field {np.count_nonzero(y)} steps")   # 1 + Σ dilations: 1,024 and 11
Ready to use Python · PyTorch
import torch
import torch.nn.functional as F
from torch import nn

class GatedResidual(nn.Module):
    """One WaveNet layer: dilated causal conv, gated activation, residual connection."""
    def __init__(self, channels, dilation, K=2):
        super().__init__()
        self.pad = (K - 1) * dilation
        self.conv = nn.Conv1d(channels, 2 * channels, K, dilation=dilation)    # filter and gate in one conv
        self.mix = nn.Conv1d(channels, channels, 1)

    def forward(self, x):                                                     # x: (batch, channels, time)
        f, g = self.conv(F.pad(x, (self.pad, 0))).chunk(2, dim=1)            # pad on the left only
        return x + self.mix(torch.tanh(f) * torch.sigmoid(g))

net = nn.Sequential(*[GatedResidual(32, 2 ** l) for l in range(10)])
x = torch.randn(1, 32, 2048, requires_grad=True)
net(x)[0, :, 1500].sum().backward()              # which input steps can change output step 1500?
seen = x.grad.abs().sum(1)[0].nonzero().flatten()
print(seen.min().item(), seen.max().item(), len(seen))   # 477 ... 1500: 1,024 steps, nothing after 1500

The encoder-decoder transformer and its three families Expert

The first transformer (Vaswani and others, 2017) translated sentences, so it kept the encoder-decoder layout of seq2seq models () and replaced the RNNs with attention (). The encoder is a stack of $N = 6$ blocks (), each with self-attention and a feed-forward network, and it reads the whole source sentence at once. Each decoder block has three sublayers: masked self-attention over the words written so far, cross-attention, and a feed-forward network. In cross-attention the queries come from the decoder, and the keys and values come from the encoder's final output $H$:

$$\operatorname{CrossAttn}(Y, H) = \operatorname{softmax}\!\Big(\frac{(YW_Q)(HW_K)^\top}{\sqrt{d_k}}\Big)\,HW_V$$

Each attention gets its own mask. In the encoder, each source token sees each source token. In the decoder's self-attention, target token $i$ sees targets 1 to $i$. In cross-attention, each target token sees each source token. The base model used $d = 512$, 8 heads and a feed-forward width of 2,048: about 65 million parameters.

Later models kept one half or both, and you can tell them apart by their masks:

  • Encoder-only (BERT, ): full attention over the input. Good for classification, tagging and embeddings for search.
  • Decoder-only (GPT, Llama): one causal stack reads the prompt and writes the answer as a single sequence. Good for generation; it scales well, and most large language models use it.
  • Encoder-decoder (T5, BART, Whisper): full attention over the input, causal attention over the output, and cross-attention between them. Good for tasks with a clear input and output, such as translation, summarization and speech recognition.

A prefix language model sits between the last two: one stack, with full attention inside the prompt and causal attention after it.

Analogy. Translation by a two-person team. The reader (the encoder) studies the whole English page and takes notes. The writer (the decoder) writes the French text word by word, rereading their own draft (masked self-attention) and checking the reader's notes (cross-attention). A decoder-only model is one person who reads the page aloud and keeps talking; an encoder-only model reads the page and answers questions about it but writes no prose.

Attention masks for translating "the black cat sleeps" into "le chat noir dort". Each row is a query token and each colored cell a key it may read. Pick an architecture to see its masks, and click a row to list what that token reads.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
d = 16

def attend(Q, K, V, mask):                       # mask[i, j] = True: query i may see key j
    s = np.where(mask, Q @ K.T / np.sqrt(d), -np.inf)
    w = np.exp(s - s.max(1, keepdims=True))
    return (w / w.sum(1, keepdims=True)) @ V

W = {name: rng.normal(0, d ** -0.5, (3, d, d)) for name in ("enc", "self", "cross")}   # Q, K, V maps per sublayer

def encoder(X):                                  # full self-attention over the source
    Wq, Wk, Wv = W["enc"]
    return X + attend(X @ Wq, X @ Wk, X @ Wv, np.ones((len(X), len(X)), bool))

def decoder(Y, H):
    n = len(Y)
    Wq, Wk, Wv = W["self"]
    Y = Y + attend(Y @ Wq, Y @ Wk, Y @ Wv, np.tril(np.ones((n, n), bool)))    # causal self-attention
    Wq, Wk, Wv = W["cross"]
    return Y + attend(Y @ Wq, H @ Wk, H @ Wv, np.ones((n, len(H)), bool))      # cross-attention: keys and values from H

src, tgt = rng.normal(size=(4, d)), rng.normal(size=(5, d))   # 4 source tokens, 5 target tokens
H = encoder(src)
out = decoder(tgt, H)
tgt2 = tgt.copy()
tgt2[4] += 10.0                                  # change the last target token
print(out.shape, np.allclose(decoder(tgt2, H)[:4], out[:4]))   # True: earlier positions never see later ones
Ready to use Python · PyTorch
import torch
from torch import nn

model = nn.Transformer(d_model=512, nhead=8, num_encoder_layers=6, num_decoder_layers=6,
                       dim_feedforward=2048, batch_first=True)          # the base model of the 2017 paper
src, tgt = torch.randn(2, 10, 512), torch.randn(2, 7, 512)            # embedded source and target, batch of 2
tgt_mask = nn.Transformer.generate_square_subsequent_mask(7)          # causal mask: -inf above the diagonal
print(model(src, tgt, tgt_mask=tgt_mask).shape)                       # (2, 7, 512)
blocks = sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters())
print(f"{blocks:,} in the blocks + {37000 * 512:,} in a shared 37,000-token embedding")   # about 63 M in total
# Pretrained encoder-decoder models: AutoModelForSeq2SeqLM.from_pretrained("t5-small") in transformers

Inside a modern LLM: the Llama block Expert

The GPT-2 block of 2019 () has changed in four places since. Llama (Touvron and others, 2023) collected the changes, and most open models share them. The block stays pre-norm, $\mathbf x \leftarrow \mathbf x + \operatorname{Attn}(\operatorname{Norm}(\mathbf x))$ followed by $\mathbf x \leftarrow \mathbf x + \operatorname{FFN}(\operatorname{Norm}(\mathbf x))$, and the parts inside change.

RMSNorm replaces LayerNorm (). It skips the mean subtraction and the bias and divides by the root mean square: $\operatorname{RMSNorm}(\mathbf x) = \mathbf g\odot\mathbf x\big/\sqrt{\tfrac1d\sum_i x_i^2 + \epsilon}$. It costs less and trains as well. RoPE () rotates the queries and keys by position, in place of position vectors added to the input. SwiGLU replaces the ReLU or GELU feed-forward network with a gated one:

$$\operatorname{FFN}(\mathbf x) = W_2\big(\operatorname{SiLU}(W_1\mathbf x)\odot W_3\mathbf x\big), \qquad \operatorname{SiLU}(z) = z\,\sigma(z)$$

Three matrices replace two, so the hidden width drops from $4d$ to about $\frac83d$ to keep the parameter count. Llama also drops the biases from its linear layers.

Grouped-query attention (GQA) lets several query heads share one key head and one value head. With $h$ query heads and $g$ key-value heads, each group of $h/g$ query heads reads the same keys and values; $g = h$ gives ordinary multi-head attention, and $g = 1$ gives multi-query attention. The saving shows up in the KV cache (), which stores the keys and values of each past token at each layer. For $L$ layers, head size $d_{\text{head}}$, $n$ tokens and $b$ bytes per number:

$$\text{KV cache} = 2\cdot L\cdot g\cdot d_{\text{head}}\cdot n\cdot b \ \text{bytes}$$

Llama 3 8B has $L = 32$, $h = 32$, $g = 8$ and $d_{\text{head}} = 128$. In 16-bit numbers its cache takes 128 KiB per token and 1 GiB for an 8,192-token context, four times less than with $g = 32$. During generation the GPU spends most of its time reading the weights and this cache from memory, so a smaller cache lets one GPU serve more users or longer contexts.

Analogy. A class of 32 students (query heads) studies from textbooks (keys and values). With multi-head attention each student owns a copy. With grouped-query attention, groups of four share a copy: each student still asks their own questions, and the school buys 8 books instead of 32.

Top: query heads, colored by the key-value head they share. Bottom: memory for the weights and for the KV cache. Pick a model, then change $g$, the context length and the number of sequences served at once. With long contexts and many users, the cache outgrows the model.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
d, h, g, dh, ff = 64, 8, 2, 8, 172               # width, query heads, key-value heads, head size, FFN width ≈ 8d/3

def rms_norm(x, eps=1e-6):                       # the learned gain starts at 1, so it is left out here
    return x / np.sqrt((x ** 2).mean(-1, keepdims=True) + eps)

def rope(x, pos, base=10000.0):                  # rotate each coordinate pair by the angle pos·θ_i
    theta = base ** (-np.arange(0, dh, 2) / dh)
    c, s = np.cos(pos[:, None] * theta), np.sin(pos[:, None] * theta)
    out = np.empty_like(x)
    out[..., 0::2] = x[..., 0::2] * c - x[..., 1::2] * s
    out[..., 1::2] = x[..., 0::2] * s + x[..., 1::2] * c
    return out

Wq, Wo = rng.normal(0, d ** -0.5, (2, d, d))
Wk, Wv = rng.normal(0, d ** -0.5, (2, d, g * dh))      # g = 2 key-value heads: 4 times fewer columns than Wq
W1, W3 = rng.normal(0, d ** -0.5, (2, d, ff))
W2 = rng.normal(0, ff ** -0.5, (ff, d))
silu = lambda z: z / (1 + np.exp(-z))

def block(x, pos, cache):
    """x: (n, d) new tokens at positions pos. cache keeps the keys and values of all earlier tokens."""
    a = rms_norm(x)
    heads = lambda m, k: m.reshape(-1, k, dh).transpose(1, 0, 2)
    q, k, v = rope(heads(a @ Wq, h), pos), rope(heads(a @ Wk, g), pos), heads(a @ Wv, g)
    cache["k"], cache["v"] = np.concatenate([cache["k"], k], 1), np.concatenate([cache["v"], v], 1)
    K, V = np.repeat(cache["k"], h // g, 0), np.repeat(cache["v"], h // g, 0)   # each KV head serves h/g query heads
    s = q @ K.transpose(0, 2, 1) / np.sqrt(dh)
    s = np.where(np.arange(K.shape[1]) > pos[:, None], -np.inf, s)            # causal mask
    w = np.exp(s - s.max(-1, keepdims=True))
    x = x + ((w / w.sum(-1, keepdims=True)) @ V).transpose(1, 0, 2).reshape(-1, d) @ Wo
    b = rms_norm(x)
    return x + (silu(b @ W1) * (b @ W3)) @ W2                                  # SwiGLU feed-forward

empty = lambda: {"k": np.zeros((g, 0, dh)), "v": np.zeros((g, 0, dh))}
x = rng.normal(size=(6, d))
full = block(x, np.arange(6), empty())                                         # six tokens in one pass
cache = empty()
steps = np.vstack([block(x[t:t + 1], np.array([t]), cache) for t in range(6)])   # one token at a time, reusing the cache
print(np.allclose(full, steps), "| cache holds", cache["k"].size + cache["v"].size, "numbers; with g = h it would hold", 2 * 6 * h * dh)
Ready to use Python · Hugging Face transformers
import torch
from transformers import LlamaConfig, LlamaForCausalLM

config = LlamaConfig(vocab_size=1000, hidden_size=256, intermediate_size=688, num_hidden_layers=4,
                     num_attention_heads=8, num_key_value_heads=2)
model = LlamaForCausalLM(config)                       # random weights, nothing to download
print(model.model.layers[0])                           # q/k/v/o projections, SwiGLU MLP (gate, up, down), two RMSNorms
print(sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters()) / 1e6, "M parameters")
ids = torch.randint(0, 1000, (1, 8))
print(model.generate(ids, max_new_tokens=5, do_sample=False).shape)   # generate() fills and reuses the KV cache
# Trained weights: AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained("meta-llama/Llama-3.2-1B"), after you accept the license on Hugging Face

Mixture of experts Expert

A dense transformer runs each token through all of its weights. A mixture-of-experts (MoE) layer replaces the feed-forward network () with $E$ separate networks, the experts, plus a router that sends each token to a few of them. The router is a linear layer followed by softmax (). The layer keeps the top $k$ experts and mixes their outputs by the router weights, renormalized over the chosen ones:

$$\mathbf p = \operatorname{softmax}(W_r\mathbf x), \qquad \mathcal T = \operatorname{top}_k(\mathbf p), \qquad \mathbf y = \sum_{i\in\mathcal T}\frac{p_i}{\sum_{j\in\mathcal T}p_j}\,E_i(\mathbf x)$$

The layer stores the weights of all $E$ experts but runs $k$ of them per token. Mixtral 8x7B (2024) has 8 experts per layer and picks $k = 2$. It stores 47 billion parameters and uses 13 billion per token, so it runs at the cost of a 13-billion-parameter dense model. The attention layers stay shared.

Routing can collapse. The router finds that a few experts work, sends them more tokens, they improve, and the other experts starve. Switch Transformer (Fedus and others, 2021), which routes each token to one expert, adds a load-balancing loss over a batch of $T$ tokens:

$$L_{\text{aux}} = \alpha\,E\sum_{i=1}^{E} f_i\,P_i, \qquad f_i = \frac{\#\{\text{tokens sent to } i\}}{T}, \qquad P_i = \frac1T\sum_{\text{tokens}} p_i$$

It reaches its minimum, $\alpha$, when each expert gets the same share of the tokens and of the router probability. Each expert also has a capacity of $\text{capacity factor}\times kT/E$ tokens per batch, so the work fits fixed-size buffers on the GPU. Tokens beyond it skip that layer and pass through on the residual connection. DeepSeek-V3 (2024) keeps the extra loss tiny and balances with a bias: it adds a bias to each expert's score when choosing the top $k$, raises the bias of underloaded experts after each step and lowers the bias of overloaded ones.

Analogy. A hospital with eight specialists and a triage nurse. The nurse (the router) reads each patient's symptoms and sends them to two specialists. The hospital pays all eight (stored parameters), but each patient sees two (active parameters). If the nurse sends all the patients to the cardiologist, a queue forms there while the others sit idle; load balancing keeps the queues even.

Left: tokens in a 2D embedding space, colored by their first-choice expert. The arrows are the router's weight vectors, and a token's score for an expert is its dot product with that arrow. Right: tokens per expert against the capacity line. Drag the arrows to change the routing, or press Balance to run DeepSeek-style bias updates and watch the loads even out.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
T, d, E, k, ff = 64, 16, 8, 2, 32                # tokens, width, experts, experts per token, expert FFN width
x = rng.normal(size=(T, d))
Wr = rng.normal(0, 0.5, (d, E))                  # router
experts = [(rng.normal(0, d ** -0.5, (d, ff)), rng.normal(0, ff ** -0.5, (ff, d))) for _ in range(E)]

logits = x @ Wr
p = np.exp(logits - logits.max(1, keepdims=True))
p /= p.sum(1, keepdims=True)                                          # router probabilities
top = np.argsort(-p, axis=1)[:, :k]                                   # the k best experts per token
gate = np.take_along_axis(p, top, 1)
gate /= gate.sum(1, keepdims=True)                                    # renormalize over the chosen k

y = np.zeros_like(x)
for t in range(T):
    for i, weight in zip(top[t], gate[t]):
        W1, W2 = experts[i]
        y[t] += weight * np.maximum(0, x[t] @ W1) @ W2                # only k of the E experts run for this token

load = np.bincount(top.ravel(), minlength=E)
f, P = load / (T * k), p.mean(0)                                      # share of assignments, mean probability
capacity = int(1.25 * T * k / E)                                      # capacity factor 1.25
print("tokens per expert:", load, "| capacity", capacity, "| dropped", np.maximum(0, load - capacity).sum())
print(f"balance loss E·Σ f·P = {E * (f * P).sum():.3f} (1.000 when uniform)")
Ready to use Python · Hugging Face transformers
import torch
from transformers import MixtralConfig, MixtralForCausalLM

config = MixtralConfig(vocab_size=1000, hidden_size=128, intermediate_size=256, num_hidden_layers=2,
                       num_attention_heads=4, num_key_value_heads=2, num_local_experts=8, num_experts_per_tok=2,
                       output_router_logits=True, router_aux_loss_coef=0.01)
model = MixtralForCausalLM(config)               # random weights; trained model: "mistralai/Mixtral-8x7B-v0.1"
ids = torch.randint(0, 1000, (2, 16))
out = model(ids, labels=ids)
print(out.loss.item(), out.aux_loss.item())      # next-token loss (balance term included); balance loss, 2.0 = k when uniform
total = sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters())
in_experts = sum(p.numel() for n, p in model.named_parameters() if "experts" in n)
print(f"{total:,} stored; about {total - in_experts + in_experts * 2 // 8:,} used per token")

State space models and Mamba Expert

Attention compares each token with each other token, so its compute grows with $n^2$ and its KV cache with $n$. An RNN () carries a fixed-size state but trains one step at a time. A state space model (SSM) offers both: it trains in parallel like a CNN and generates like an RNN with constant memory. A linear SSM maps an input sequence $x_t$ to outputs $y_t$ through a state $\mathbf h_t \in \mathbb R^N$:

$$\mathbf h_t = \bar A\,\mathbf h_{t-1} + \bar B\,x_t, \qquad y_t = C\,\mathbf h_t$$

$\bar A$ and $\bar B$ come from a continuous system $\mathbf h'(s) = A\mathbf h(s) + Bx(s)$ sampled with step $\Delta$: $\bar A = e^{\Delta A}$ and $\bar B \approx \Delta B$. The recurrence has no nonlinearity, so you can unroll it, $\mathbf h_t = \sum_{j\ge0}\bar A^j\bar B\,x_{t-j}$, and the output becomes a convolution:

$$y_t = \sum_{j=0}^{t} K_j\,x_{t-j}, \qquad K = \big(C\bar B,\ C\bar A\bar B,\ C\bar A^2\bar B,\ \dots\big)$$

$K$ is a causal convolution kernel () as long as the sequence. During training you compute all outputs at once (S4 uses the FFT); during generation you run the recurrence, one cheap step per token. With a diagonal $A$, each state coordinate is a leaky memory that fades at its own rate, and $\Delta$ sets how long it lasts.

A fixed kernel treats each input the same way, so it can't decide to skip filler words or hold on to a name. Mamba (Gu and Dao, 2023) makes $\Delta$, $B$ and $C$ depend on the current input, for example $\Delta_t = \operatorname{softplus}(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_t + b)$. A large $\Delta_t$ overwrites the state with the current input; a small one keeps the state and ignores the input, like an LSTM's forget gate (). The convolution view no longer holds, so Mamba computes the recurrence with a parallel scan that keeps the state in fast on-chip memory, and its cost grows in proportion to $n$. Gu and Dao report that a 3-billion-parameter Mamba beats transformers of the same size on language modeling. Hybrids such as Jamba mix Mamba layers with a few attention layers.

Analogy. Attention keeps a full transcript of the meeting and rereads it for each new question. An SSM keeps a one-page notebook and updates it after each sentence. A fixed SSM updates the page the same way whatever anyone says; Mamba's note-taker decides, sentence by sentence, whether to rewrite the page or skip the sentence.

A one-state SSM ($A = -1$, $B = C = 1$, exact zero-order-hold sampling). Top: an input with three marked tokens among filler noise. Middle: the fixed SSM's output from the recurrence (line) and from the convolution (dots), which agree; in red, a selective SSM whose $\Delta_t$ is large on the marked tokens and tiny elsewhere, as a trained Mamba layer could learn. Bottom: the kernel $K$. Change $\Delta$ to trade memory length against noise.

From scratch Python · NumPy
import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
N, T = 4, 200
A = -np.array([0.05, 0.2, 0.8, 2.0])             # diagonal A: four memories that fade at different rates
B, C = rng.normal(size=N), rng.normal(size=N)
x = rng.normal(size=T)

def discretize(delta):
    return np.exp(delta * A), delta * B          # Ā = e^{ΔA}, B̄ ≈ ΔB

Ab, Bb = discretize(0.5)
h, y_rec = np.zeros(N), []
for xt in x:                                     # recurrent view: a fixed-size state, one step per token
    h = Ab * h + Bb * xt
    y_rec.append(C @ h)
K = np.array([C @ (Ab ** j * Bb) for j in range(T)])   # kernel K_j = C Ā^j B̄
y_conv = np.convolve(x, K)[:T]                         # convolutional view: all positions at once
print("recurrence == convolution:", np.allclose(y_rec, y_conv))

# Selective SSM (Mamba): Δ depends on the input, so each step decides to keep or overwrite the state.
w, b = 2.0, -1.0
h, y_sel = np.zeros(N), []
for xt in x:
    Ab, Bb = discretize(np.log1p(np.exp(w * xt + b)))   # Δ_t = softplus(w x_t + b)
    h = Ab * h + Bb * xt
    y_sel.append(C @ h)
print(np.round(y_sel[:6], 3))                            # no fixed kernel K produces this output
Ready to use Python · Hugging Face transformers
import torch
from transformers import MambaConfig, MambaForCausalLM

config = MambaConfig(vocab_size=1000, hidden_size=128, state_size=16, num_hidden_layers=4)
model = MambaForCausalLM(config)                 # random weights; trained: "state-spaces/mamba-130m-hf"
print(model.backbone.layers[0].mixer)            # x_proj makes Δ, B and C from each token: the selective part
ids = torch.randint(0, 1000, (1, 32))
out = model(ids, labels=ids)
print(out.loss.item(), sum(p.numel() for p in model.parameters()) / 1e6, "M parameters")
print(model.generate(ids, max_new_tokens=8, do_sample=False).shape)   # carries a fixed-size state, no KV cache
# On a GPU, pip install mamba-ssm causal-conv1d for the fast scan kernels

Vision-language models: LLaVA Expert

A vision-language model answers questions about images. LLaVA (Liu and others, 2023) builds one from parts you have met: a pretrained CLIP image encoder (), which is a ViT (), and a pretrained decoder-only language model (). A small projector connects them. The ViT turns the image into one vector per patch, the projector (a two-layer MLP in LLaVA-1.5) maps each vector into the language model's embedding space, and the language model reads the results as tokens placed before the text:

$$Z = \operatorname{ViT}(\text{image}) \in \mathbb R^{N\times d_v}, \qquad H_v = \operatorname{MLP}(Z) \in \mathbb R^{N\times d}, \qquad \text{input} = \big[H_v;\ \operatorname{Embed}(\text{text})\big]$$

In LLaVA-1.5, a 336 × 336 image cut into 14 × 14 patches gives $N = 24^2 = 576$ image tokens of width $d_v = 1{,}024$, and the projector maps them to the language model's $d = 4{,}096$. Training has two stages. First you freeze both pretrained models and train the projector alone on image-caption pairs, so image tokens land where the language model expects words. Then you unfreeze the language model and fine-tune it with the projector on visual instruction data: images paired with questions and answers.

Image tokens use up context. Small text needs high resolution, so later models split a large image into tiles and encode each one. LLaVA-NeXT uses up to four 336 × 336 tiles plus a downscaled overview, 2,880 tokens. Qwen2-VL merges each 2 × 2 group of neighboring patches into one token, which cuts the count by four.

Analogy. An interpreter between two experts who don't share a language. The photographer (the vision encoder) describes the picture in their own vocabulary, the interpreter (the projector) translates each description into the writer's language, and the writer (the language model) answers questions as if it had read a text about the picture. Training first teaches the interpreter alone, then lets the writer adapt.

The grid on the image shows the ViT's patches. Change the resolution and the patch size, merge 2 × 2 patches, or switch to tiles, and watch how much of the context window the image takes before the question starts.

From scratch Python · PyTorch
import torch
from torch import nn

d_v, d, vocab = 64, 128, 1000
vision = nn.Sequential(nn.Conv2d(3, d_v, 14, stride=14), nn.Flatten(2))   # patch embedding; a real ViT adds attention layers
projector = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_v, d), nn.GELU(), nn.Linear(d, d))  # LLaVA-1.5's two-layer MLP
embed = nn.Embedding(vocab, d)
lm = nn.TransformerEncoder(nn.TransformerEncoderLayer(d, 4, 4 * d, batch_first=True, norm_first=True), 2)
head = nn.Linear(d, vocab)

def forward(image, text_ids):
    z = vision(image).transpose(1, 2)                         # (batch, N patches, d_v)
    seq = torch.cat([projector(z), embed(text_ids)], dim=1)   # image tokens first, then the text tokens
    mask = nn.Transformer.generate_square_subsequent_mask(seq.shape[1])
    return head(lm(seq, mask=mask, is_causal=True))           # next-token scores at each position

image, text = torch.randn(1, 3, 336, 336), torch.randint(0, vocab, (1, 20))
print(forward(image, text).shape)                             # (1, 576 + 20, 1000): 24² = 576 image tokens
for part in (vision, embed, lm, head):                        # stage 1: train the projector alone
    part.requires_grad_(False)
print(sum(p.numel() for p in projector.parameters() if p.requires_grad), "trainable parameters in stage 1")
Ready to use Python · Hugging Face transformers
import torch
from transformers import CLIPVisionConfig, LlamaConfig, LlavaConfig, LlavaForConditionalGeneration

config = LlavaConfig(
    vision_config=CLIPVisionConfig(image_size=336, patch_size=14, hidden_size=64, intermediate_size=128,
                                   num_hidden_layers=2, num_attention_heads=4),
    text_config=LlamaConfig(vocab_size=1000, hidden_size=128, intermediate_size=256, num_hidden_layers=2,
                            num_attention_heads=4),
    image_token_id=999)
model = LlavaForConditionalGeneration(config)    # a tiny random LLaVA; the real one is "llava-hf/llava-1.5-7b-hf" (about 14 GB)
print(model.model.multi_modal_projector)         # Linear, GELU, Linear
n_img = (336 // 14) ** 2                         # 576 placeholder tokens, one per patch
ids = torch.cat([torch.full((1, n_img), 999), torch.randint(0, 998, (1, 12))], dim=1)
out = model(input_ids=ids, pixel_values=torch.randn(1, 3, 336, 336))
print(out.logits.shape)                          # (1, 588, 1000)

Check yourself. Answer each question before you open it.

What is the receptive field of 10 causal convolution layers with kernel size 2 and dilations 1, 2, 4, …, 512?

$1 + (1 + 2 + \dots + 512) = 1{,}024$ steps.

Which mask does a decoder-only model use, and how does a prefix LM differ?

A causal mask over the whole sequence. A prefix LM lets the prompt tokens see each other in both directions and keeps the causal mask after the prompt.

How much smaller is Llama 3 8B's KV cache with $g = 8$ key-value heads than it would be with $g = 32$?

Four times smaller, since the cache grows in proportion to $g$: 128 KiB per token instead of 512 KiB.

Mixtral 8x7B stores 47 billion parameters. Why does it run at the cost of a 13-billion-parameter model?

Each token passes through 2 of the 8 experts in each layer, and all tokens share the attention layers, so about 13 billion parameters do work for each token.

Why can a linear state space model train like a CNN and generate like an RNN?

Its recurrence unrolls into a convolution with kernel $K_j = C\bar A^j\bar B$, which you compute for all positions at once in training. To generate, you run the recurrence with a fixed-size state.
